<a id="feedback"></a>

## Feedback & Bug Reports

Found something broken, or want the app to do something it doesn't? Both are genuinely wanted — beta reports directly shape what gets built next. The **Feedback** button in the header offers every route, or use these links directly:

- **[Report a bug](https://github.com/mtnmanak/mountainmanrockets-feedback/issues/new?template=bug-report.yml)** — what you did, what you expected, what happened instead. The app version (next to the logo) and your browser help a lot; attaching the .ork file (zipped) makes most fixes far faster. Only attach designs you're comfortable sharing publicly.
- **[Request a feature](https://github.com/mtnmanak/mountainmanrockets-feedback/issues/new?template=feature-request.yml)** — describe the real task it would help with; the use case shapes the design more than the feature description does.
- **[Browse open issues](https://github.com/mtnmanak/mountainmanrockets-feedback/issues?q=is%3Aopen%20label%3Atool%3Ammrocket-sim)** — see what's already filed before adding a duplicate. The tracker covers mountainmanrockets.com and all of its online tools; a dropdown routes your report to the right one.
- **No GitHub account?** Email [admin@mountainmanrockets.com](mailto:admin@mountainmanrockets.com) and it gets filed for you — you don't need an account to *read* anything on the tracker.

The open-issues list is the real queue — anything filed there is logged, visible, and not forgotten.

**Your files stay yours.** Design files, flight recordings and screenshots you send in — by email or on the forum — are used to reproduce what you reported and to check the app's predictions against real flights. They are not published, passed on, or added to any public collection. If a comparison drawn from your file is worth showing others, you are asked first and credited the way you prefer. Anything you attach to a public GitHub issue is, of course, public — attach there only what you are comfortable sharing.

---

<a id="limitations-and-references"></a>

## Assumptions, Limitations & References

The physics above is powerful, but every simulation makes modeling choices. Knowing them tells you exactly where to trust the numbers and where to add engineering margin.

## Assumptions and honest limitations

- **Speed regime.** Extended Barrowman is at its best **subsonic and through the transonic region**. Fins get a genuine supersonic treatment, but body CNα and CP above Mach 1 are assumed equal to their subsonic values, and the engine raises a *supersonic* warning past Mach 1.1. Treat high-supersonic stability numbers as indicative, not precise — the Drag analysis chart labels its values above ~Mach 1.5 as approximate for the same reason.
- **Small-angle / attached-flow aerodynamics.** The method assumes slender bodies and largely attached flow. Fin normal force is capped at a **20° stall angle** — past that, more angle of attack buys no more fin force. A separate **17.5°** threshold flags the flight, before any recovery device opens: a large angle-of-attack warning while the rocket is still stable, a transition to tumbling once it is not. The body-lift term is damped below Mach 0.05 at angles of attack over 45° — the apogee turnaround — to avoid an artifact there. Extreme angles of attack are approximations.
- **Descent is a point mass.** Under parachute the rocket is 3DOF: drag (default Cd 0.8) and gravity only. Swinging, spilling, and canopy dynamics are not modeled, and **streamers** — especially at extreme length-to-width ratios — are represented by an effective drag area, not by true flexible-body aerodynamics.
- **Wind is horizontal and altitude-uniform** in the standard model; real wind shear and vertical gusts are not captured here.
- **Geodesy approximations.** The altitude gravity correction assumes a spherical Earth; the atmosphere drifts a few percent above ~32 km.
- **The thrust curve is trusted verbatim.** Motor-to-motor manufacturing variation, temperature effects on propellant, and off-axis thrust are outside the model.
- **The thrust curve is also assumed to be a sea-level curve.** Neither the RASP (`.eng`) nor the RSE (`.rse`) file format has a field for the pressure a curve was fired at, so the app adds the exact pressure-thrust rise above the pad but cannot remove the constant offset in a curve that was measured on a mountain — at most about 1.4 % of a J540R for a 1 inch exit fired at 1,300 m (see *How It Works: Physics & Math → Motor thrust-curve model*). A wrongly typed nozzle exit diameter is a much larger error than that one.

None of these are bugs — they are the modeling choices of a mature, widely validated tool, and that tool's kernel is genuinely what computes your flight, so its accuracy *and* its limits come with it. **Classic Extended Barrowman** is that kernel's own aerodynamics with none of the app's additions switched on, which is what makes it the baseline. What the app adds on top of it — the Kbf body carryover, the sharp-airfoil leading-edge treatment, the validated supersonic model, and the nozzle's power-on base drag and pressure-thrust terms — brings accuracy and limits of its own, described in *How It Works: Physics & Math*.

## Licensing and source code

MMRocket Sim is **free software**, released under the **GNU General Public License, version 3 or later (GPL v3+)** — the same license as OpenRocket itself, which it inherits. You are free to use, study, share, and modify it under that license. Because the GPL requires that anyone running a distributed build be offered its corresponding source code, the app's header carries a **source (GPL)** link — titled *"This app is free software under the GPL v3 or later — source code for this build"* — that opens the public source repository. If you want to see exactly what the app does, that link is the front door.

The weather that **☁ Get weather** fetches is data from [Open-Meteo.com](https://open-meteo.com/), used under the [Creative Commons Attribution 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/) licence, and its place search uses [GeoNames](https://www.geonames.org/) data under the same licence. The app credits Open-Meteo wherever it shows their numbers — the weather dialog, the strip beside applied weather, and the Fly screen — and adds GeoNames in each of those places whenever the place was found by a search. What the app does with those numbers — the choice of the altitude they are fetched for, the review, and any value marked as the app's own estimate — is the app's, not theirs.

## References & further reading

- **Barrowman, J. S., & Barrowman, J. A. (1966).** *The Practical Calculation of the Aerodynamic Characteristics of Slender Finned Vehicles.* (J. S. Barrowman, M.S. thesis, The Catholic University of America, 1967.) The origin of the center-of-pressure and normal-force method used here.
- **Niskanen, S. (2009, rev. 2013).** *Development of an Open Source model rocket simulation software* / *OpenRocket technical documentation.* The definitive derivation of OpenRocket's extended Barrowman aerodynamics, drag build-up, mass model and simulation loop. https://openrocket.info/documentation.html
- **Galejs, R. (2006).** *Wind instability — what Barrowman left out.* Sport Rocketry / online. Source of the body-lift extension applied to long airframes.
- **NASA (1963).** *Collection of Zero-Lift Drag Data on Bodies of Revolution from Free-Flight Investigations,* NASA TR-R-100 (NTRS 19630004995). Source of the nose-cone pressure-drag data tables.
- **Pitts, W. C., Nielsen, J. N., & Kaattari, G. E. (1957).** *Lift and Center of Pressure of Wing-Body-Tail Combinations at Subsonic, Transonic, and Supersonic Speeds,* NACA Report 1307. Source of the optional body-in-presence-of-fins (Kbf) interference factor.
- **Rogers, C. E., & Cooper, D. (2011).** *RASAero Aerodynamic Analysis and Flight Simulation Program.* Rogers Aeroscience. Documents the power-on/power-off drag distinction, the sea-level-referenced pressure-thrust correction, and the Rogers Modified Barrowman method that inspired those options here.
- **International Standard Atmosphere:** ISO 2533:1975; and the *U.S. Standard Atmosphere, 1976* (NOAA/NASA/USAF). Basis of the temperature, pressure and density model.
- **WGS84 / Somigliana gravity formula:** *Department of Defense World Geodetic System 1984,* NIMA TR8350.2. Basis of the latitude- and altitude-dependent gravity model.
- **Runge–Kutta integration:** Butcher, J. C., *Numerical Methods for Ordinary Differential Equations;* or Press et al., *Numerical Recipes,* ch. 16. The RK4 scheme used for the equations of motion.
- **Open-Meteo:** *Free Weather API* — forecasts, the historical-weather (ERA5 reanalysis) archive, a terrain-elevation model and geocoding. https://open-meteo.com/ . Data licensed under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/). Source of everything **☁ Get weather** offers.
- **GeoNames:** geographical database, https://www.geonames.org/ , licensed under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0. The place names, coordinates and heights behind the weather dialog's town and postal-code search.
- **OpenRocket project:** https://openrocket.info — source at https://github.com/openrocket/openrocket. Licensed under the **GNU General Public License v3.0-or-later**: https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-3.0.html . MMRocket Sim inherits this license.

<a id="glossary"></a>

## Glossary

This glossary explains the words the app shows you — on the design editor's fields, in the motor browser, on the **Launch conditions** panel, and in the results, plots and downloads — and the terms this guide's physics sections use. Each entry gives a plain definition first, then where you meet the term in the app (*In the app:*), then, where it matters, what to watch for. A name in bold after "See" is another entry in this glossary. The guide cannot jump from one entry to another for you, so open this section and use your browser's find (Ctrl+F, or ⌘F on a Mac) to go straight to a term. File types, and the symbols used in plots and exports, have their own tables at the end.

### A

**Acceleration** — How quickly velocity changes, in speed or in direction. *In the app:* the **Acceleration** plot under **Flight plots** and the **Acceleration** column of **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** give the size of the rocket's true acceleration relative to the ground, with gravity already taken into account — the rate its velocity is really changing. The **Max accel** tile on Results, **Max acceleration** under **Show all details** and **Accel (Gs)** in the **⬇ Run table (.csv)** and **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)** downloads give its peak up to the first recovery deployment, so they leave out any spike the plot shows as a canopy opens. It reads zero while the rocket sits on the pad and close to zero under a parachute at a steady descent. An onboard accelerometer measures something different: it reads about 1 G sitting on the pad and about 1 G under a steady canopy, so your flight computer's log can sit up to about 1 G away from the app's curve. The download's **Vertical acceleration** column is the upward (+) or downward (−) part alone. You pick m/s², ft/s² or **G** under Preferences → **Units of measure** → **Acceleration**; that G is standard gravity, a fixed 9.80665 m/s², while the download's **Gravitational acceleration** column gives the local value along the flight. See **g / G (g-force)**.

**Adapter** — A sleeve that lets a smaller-diameter motor fly in a larger motor mount. *In the app:* the motor browser's **Diameter** chips offer every class at or below your mount's (and up to 1 mm above it), because a smaller motor can ride in an adapter. That is a fit by diameter alone, so check that your adapter, its retention and the length available suit the motor. The catalogue motor weight leaves the adapter out: weigh the rocket ready to fly and type it into **Weighed pad mass with this motor**, or model the adapter as parts. See **Weighed pad mass** and *Motors → Diameter-class fit (adapter-down)*.

**Aerodynamic length** — The nose-to-tail span of the rocket's external, aerodynamic parts in the configuration you are flying. *In the app:* it is the length behind the whole-rocket Reynolds number and behind your margin as a percentage — Preferences → Display → **Stability shown as** → **% of length**, and the **Stability (of length)** tile in **All stats**. The Drag analysis panel's center-of-pressure chart has a **% of length** button too, but that view plots where the CP sits rather than the margin, and divides by the overall length of every active part — the length the **Length** tile shows. The two lengths differ only when an internal part, such as a motor mount tube, centering ring, parachute or mass component, reaches past the airframe. Aerodynamic length also differs from the diameter-based **Reference length** (Lr) column in the flight-data download. See **Reference length, Lr / reference diameter**.

**Aerodynamics model** — The set of calculations that predicts the aerodynamic forces and moments on your rocket. *In the app:* Preferences → Aerodynamics → **Aerodynamics model** offers **Classic Extended Barrowman**, **Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf)** (the default), **Auto** and **Supersonic**; the vitals strip's **Aero** selector offers the same four for the current session only, and **Batch simulate motors…** has its own **aero model** choice, starting on **Auto (recommended)**. One model flies the whole flight, and each saved run names it in its **Aero model** row. Opening a `.ork`, `.rkt` or `.CDX1` file leaves your choice as it is. See the entry for each model.

**Aft / fore** — Toward the tail / toward the nose. These name positions on the rocket whichever way it is pointing. *In the app:* you meet them in labels such as a camera shroud's **Fore end (toward the nose)** and **Aft end (toward the tail)**, and **Motor overhang (past aft end)**. Moving the CP aft raises your stability margin as long as the CG stays put, and moving the CG aft lowers it.

**Aft view** — The rocket seen end-on from behind, straight up its axis, so a fin count, fin clocking, pod ring or motor cluster shows as it really sits. *In the app:* press **Aft** beside **2D** and **3D** above the Design workspace's drawing; Motors & Launch also shows it, beneath the side view in the **Rocket — motors drawn to scale** panel. Zoom with the wheel or **+** and **−**, fit with **⤢**, and once zoomed in, drag or use the arrow keys to pan. It shares the side view's roll slider, and zero points straight up for every part's clock angle. A cluster's pattern follows its own rule, so check a **Cluster rotation** here, where the tubes are drawn where the simulation flies them. See **Angle around body / clock angle**.

**Air density, ρ** — The mass of air in each cubic meter, in kg/m³. It falls as the air gets hotter or the pressure lower, and humid air is slightly lighter than dry air at the same temperature and pressure. Density scales every aerodynamic force and sets how fast a parachute comes down, so the same rocket flies and descends differently at two launch sites. *In the app:* **Temperature** and **Station pressure** in **Launch conditions** set it at the pad (blank boxes fly the standard air for your **Site altitude**); **Density altitude** sums it up, and the flight-data download's **Air density** column follows it through the flight. The app's atmosphere is dry air, so on a hot, humid day expect the rocket to climb a little higher and come down a little faster than shown. See **Density altitude**.

**Airfoil / fin cross-section** — The shape of a fin cut through its thickness, as distinct from its side outline, the planform. *In the app:* the **Cross section** menu on trapezoidal, elliptical and freeform fin sets offers **Square**, **Rounded** and **Airfoil (pointed)**, and your choice changes both the drag the app computes and the fins' computed weight: the app weighs an **Airfoil (pointed)** fin at 85 % and a **Rounded** one at 99 % of the same outline with square edges (the tab, and any fillet from an imported file, at full weight), so the section moves your mass and CG as well. Next to it, **Supersonic airfoil** names a RASAero-style section for fast flights — **Hexagonal**, **NACA (round LE)**, **Double wedge (diamond)**, **Biconvex**, **Hexagonal, blunt base** or **Single wedge (blunt base)** — with **LE chamfer length**, **TE chamfer length** and **LE bluntness radius** beside it; each section gets its own supersonic wave drag, and the blunt-base ones add fin base drag. The menu is on every flat fin set whatever model you fly, and its default, **Classic (from cross section)**, leaves **Cross section** in charge. A named section counts under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**; **Classic Extended Barrowman** ignores it and flies the **Cross section** alone. Under those three models, an **Airfoil (pointed)** fin left on **Classic (from cross section)** also skips the blunt leading-edge drag classic Barrowman charges, which lowers drag on a fast airfoil design. See **LE bluntness radius** and *Designing the Rocket → Fins*.

**Airframe** — The rocket's structure and everything it carries. *In the app:* in the **Measured mass & CG** box under the component tree, the airframe is the flight-ready rocket with the motor out — recovery gear and payload in — and that is what you weigh and balance for **Measured mass** and **Measured CG from nose tip**. The motor goes in for the **Weighed pad mass** on Motors & Launch. See **Dry mass / empty mass**, **Loaded mass / launch mass / pad mass**, and **Weighed pad mass**.

**Airspeed / air-relative velocity** — The rocket's speed through the surrounding air, which differs from its speed over the ground whenever the wind blows. Aerodynamic forces, Mach number and Reynolds number all use airspeed. *In the app:* the **Velocity** plot, the **Max velocity** tile and the download's **Velocity** column give speed relative to the ground, so in a wind, dividing that velocity by the download's **Speed of sound** column gives a different curve from the **Mach number** plot. See **Relative wind** and **Mach number, M**.

**Airstart** — Lighting a motor in flight, after liftoff: an upper-stage motor, or another motor on a stage that is already flying. *In the app:* set the motor's **Ignition** trigger and delay on its card in Motors & Launch — for example **Launch + delay (timer)** or **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)**. That control appears only when your design has more than one stage or a parallel booster, so a single-stage design has no airstart setting of its own. A single-stage design opened from a desktop OpenRocket `.ork` or a RockSim `.rkt` that already airstarts a motor still flies that way, though its card shows no **Ignition** control. All the tubes in one mount's **Cluster layout** share one motor choice and one ignition, so the whole cluster lights together. See **Ignition event / ignition delay** and *Multi-Stage and Clustered Rockets → Ignition triggers on a staged rocket*.

**All-stats drawer** — The Design workspace's grid of every design figure. *In the app:* headed **All stats**, it spans the bottom of the drawing; **▾ Collapse** puts it away and **▤ All stats** brings it back. It holds the length, masses, **Recovery weight**, CGs, **CP**, **Stability** in calibers and **Stability (of length)** whatever your preference, **Cd (M0.3)**, **Roll inertia (loaded)** and **Pitch inertia (loaded)**, plus **CP in this plane** when the CP depends on roll angle. In a window 981 px or wider it starts open over the drawing, puts itself away when the drawing area is under 366 px tall, and comes back once that area is over 426 px; in a narrower window it starts shut and opens as a block under the drawing. Resizing the window across 981 px opens or shuts it to match. Once you open or close it yourself, the app leaves it as you set it until you reload. See *Visualizing the Design → The 2D schematic*.

**Altimeter setting** — A pressure reduced to sea level with a standard-atmosphere calculation, so that an aircraft altimeter set to it reads the field's elevation. Meteorological sea-level pressure is a close cousin worked out slightly differently. *In the app:* the **Station pressure** field in **Launch conditions** wants the pad barometer's own reading instead. At a 3,900 ft pad that is about 878 hPa against an altimeter setting near 1,013 hPa, and typing the altimeter setting flies air about 15 % too dense, so your apogee reads low. Above a 600 m (about 2,000 ft) site, a typed sea-level-looking pressure raises a caution; clear the field to fly the standard pressure for your **Site altitude**, or let **☁ Get weather…** fetch station pressure for you. See **Station pressure**. [NWS: pressure definitions](https://www.weather.gov/bou/pressure_definitions).

**Altitude: AGL and ASL** — AGL is height above ground level; ASL is height above sea level. A flight 1,000 m above a pad at 500 m ASL is at 1,500 m ASL. *In the app:* the **Altitude** plot, every apogee the app shows, and **Deploy altitude (AGL)** — used when **Deploy at** is set to **Altitude (descending)** — all measure height above the launch site. **Site altitude** in **Launch conditions** is ASL, as is the flight-data download's **Altitude above sea level** column, and **☁ Get weather…** can fill Site altitude from a terrain model's ground height. The simulation keeps the ground at the pad's height for the whole flight, so a landing on a hillside or in a valley is worked out as if on level ground.

**Angle around body / clock angle** — Where a part sits around the rocket's circumference, read like a clock face. *In the app:* the field is **Angle around body** on launch lugs, rail buttons, camera shrouds, protuberances, pods and parallel boosters; **Rotation (about body axis)** on a fin set or tube-fin set; and **Direction around body** on an inner tube placed off the centerline. For all of them zero is the top of the airframe as the side view draws it and straight up (12 o'clock) in the **Aft view**, which is also where an unrotated fin set puts its first fin — so a part at zero sits in line with fin 1. Every one of these parts is drawn in the Aft view, so check it there; on a part that shares its tube stack with fins, **▲ on a fin** and **⟂ between fins** set the angle for you. A motor cluster's **Cluster rotation** works differently: it turns the whole pattern, and at zero some patterns have no tube at 12 o'clock — a 3-ring puts one at 3 o'clock. A clock angle also differs from a fin's **Cant angle**, which tilts it to make the rocket roll, and from the view's roll slider, which turns only the picture.

**Angle of attack, AoA or α** — The angle between the way the rocket's nose points and the way it is moving through the air. A crosswind gives the rocket an angle of attack even while it is still on the rail. Angle of attack drives normal force, CP and drag, and decides how far the small-angle approximations in the aerodynamics can be trusted. *In the app:* the **Angle of attack** plot under **Flight plots**, the download's **Angle of attack** column, and **Angle of attack at launch guide exit** under **Show all details** and in the run-table download. That exit angle is why **CP at launch guide exit** sits forward of the Design tab's still-air CP in a crosswind. See **Relative wind**.

**Angle reference** — A setting desktop OpenRocket stores on a strap-on booster ring, naming what its angle is measured from: **Relative** or **Fixed**. *In the app:* the **Angle reference** menu on a **Booster (parallel stage)**, beside **Angle around body**. The app keeps your choice and writes it back to a `.ork`, but under either choice it places the boosters at the **Angle around body** you typed, in the drawings and in the flight, so switching it moves nothing. See **Angle around body / clock angle**.

**Apogee** — The highest point of the flight. Vertical velocity passes through zero there, though the rocket can still be moving sideways. Opening a recovery device near apogee usually gives a lower opening speed than opening during a fast climb or descent. *In the app:* **Apogee** is the first Results tile and sits in the vitals strip once you have flown, and it is measured above the pad. The report's details list the same figure as **Max altitude**. **Deploy at** set to **Apogee** opens a device there, and the recommended ejection delay aims your motor's charge at it: the coast from burnout to a deployment-free apogee, rounded to a whole second. See **Ejection delay**.

**Aspect ratio** — How long and narrow a lifting surface is: span squared divided by planform area. For fins, check which span and area a formula uses — one fin, or a pair measured tip to tip — before you compare numbers. *In the app:* no field shows it; it comes from your fin's outline and span — the **Height** field on a trapezoidal or elliptical fin set, the tallest point of the drawn outline on a freeform one. The app accounts for finite span, so two fins of equal area give different lift: the taller, narrower one makes more normal force for each unit of its area. See *How It Works: Physics & Math → Aerodynamics — the Extended Barrowman method*.

**Auto (aerodynamics)** — The model setting that picks **Supersonic** or **Rogers Kbf** for you, flight by flight. *In the app:* choose **Auto** under Preferences → Aerodynamics (the option reads **Auto — Rogers Kbf, switching to our supersonic model past Mach 0.9**) or in the vitals strip's **Aero** selector. The app first flies a short trial, given a time limit of at least 10 s, including 3 s beyond a conservative estimate of the last burnout, though it can finish earlier if the flight ends; if that trial passes Mach 0.9, your whole flight flies on Supersonic, otherwise on Rogers Kbf. If the finished flight turns out to have passed Mach 0.9 after all, it is flown once more on Supersonic. One model always flies the whole flight — Auto never switches partway up. Once a flight on Auto passes Mach 0.9, the strip shows **M+** beside **Auto** and the design's stability and drag analysis use Supersonic too, until you change the design, its motors or the launch conditions. See **M+ mark**.

**Axial drag coefficient, Cda** — The coefficient for the aerodynamic drag force along the rocket's axis. It equals Cd when the rocket flies straight into the airflow and parts from it at an angle of attack: the kernel scales it up to 1.3 times Cd near 17° and down to zero side-on. *In the app:* the flight-data download gives the two separate columns, **Axial drag coefficient** (Cda) and **Drag coefficient** (Cd). See **Cd / CD / drag coefficient**.

**Axial / radial** — Along the rocket's nose-to-tail axis / outward from that axis. An axial offset moves a part forward or aft; a radial offset moves it away from the centerline. *In the app:* a part's axial place is **Position (in parent)**: **Relative to** (**Top of parent**, **Middle of parent** or **Bottom of parent**) plus **Offset**. Radial places are **Distance off centerline** on an inner tube and **Radial distance** on pods and parallel boosters, measured as **Radial reference** says — **Gap from parent surface** or **From parent centerline**, two different places for the same number, so read it before you type. See **Radial reference** and **Position relative to / Offset**.

**Azimuth / zenith angle** — Azimuth is direction around the vertical axis, like a compass bearing; zenith angle is tilt away from straight up. *In the app:* the flight-data download gives the rocket's orientation as **Lateral orientation — azimuth** (Φ), a compass bearing with 0 at north and 90° at east, and **Vertical orientation — zenith** (Θ), both in your angle unit. Despite its name, Θ is the nose's angle above the horizon, not its tilt from vertical: it reads 90° with the nose straight up and 85° off a 5° rod, so subtract it from 90° to get the zenith angle. They describe where the nose points, which can differ from the way the rocket is moving; for where it landed, read **Landing bearing** under **Show all details**.

### B

**Ballast** — Mass you add on purpose to change a rocket's total mass or its balance point. Nose weight moves the CG forward and raises your stability margin, at the price of a heavier rocket: a slower rod exit and, on most designs, a lower apogee. *In the app:* add a **Mass component** inside the nose cone, a body tube or a coupler and set its **Mass** and **Length**; it also stands in for equipment you want counted by weight alone. For a rocket you have built, **Measured mass & CG** works out the mass your model is missing from your scale and balance-point readings and adds it as a mass component, so the model matches the build. See **Build allowance**.

**Ballistic descent** — A descent with no recovery drag slowing the rocket as intended, so it falls or tumbles in fast. *In the app:* when your design holds no parachute or streamer anywhere, the flight carries the warning "No recovery device — the rocket comes down ballistic", in the report and in a red banner across Results. That warning checks only whether a device exists somewhere in the design, so one that is there but never opens leaves it silent. The fast arrival then shows as a failed **Landing rate OK (≤ 20 ft/s)** — and, for a separated booster whose motor is over 80 N average thrust or 160 N·s total impulse, as a line saying that booster "has NO recovery device". Every device that opened has its own row in the report's recovery table, so check there. Descent under an open parachute or streamer is a different case: see **Descent rate**.

**Barrowman method** — A family of engineering formulas that builds a rocket's normal force and CP from the contributions of its nose, its changes in body diameter and its fins. Extended versions add body lift at angle of attack and fin lift that changes with Mach number through the transonic and supersonic range. It sums each part's share rather than solving the airflow around the rocket, as CFD does. *In the app:* every **Aerodynamics model** builds on it — **Classic Extended Barrowman** is desktop OpenRocket 24.12's own version, **Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf)**, the default, adds the lift the fins carry onto the body, and **Supersonic** extends it to high Mach. See *How It Works: Physics & Math → Aerodynamics — the Extended Barrowman method*.

**Base drag** — Drag from the low-pressure wake behind the rocket's flat, aft-facing area: its blunt tail, or a step down to a narrower tube. It pulls straight back, adding drag and nothing to the CP or to the stabilizing normal force. *In the app:* it is worked out apart from skin friction and the other pressure drag, and shows as **Base** under **By type** in the **Drag analysis** panel's **Breakdown (power-off)** chart. Under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**, a stage's **Nozzle exit diameter** lowers it while the motor burns, because the exhaust fills part of that stage's aft base; that drop shows in the panel's dashed **CD power-on** curve rather than in the breakdown. See **Power-on / power-off drag** and **Center of pressure, CP**.

**Batch simulation** — Flying one design with a list of candidate motors, one simulated flight per row, so you can compare them. *In the app:* press **Batch simulate motors…** on Motors & Launch and set your acceptance criteria (**Min rod-exit**, **Min thrust:weight**, an apogee window); the rows that pass go into your run history. With **optimal delay per motor** ticked, the default, each row uses its simulated optimum delay rounded to a whole second when one is available; untick it and each motor normally flies the delay the motor browser would pick for it by default. A plugged-only row whose recovery needs an ejection charge uses an available optimum instead, marked **· opt.**. In the results, **· nozzle** marks a row that flew a published nozzle exit (not shown under **Classic Extended Barrowman**) and **· weighed** the row that carried your weighed pad-mass hardware. A row flies one candidate motor in every tube; on a mount whose **Cluster layout** is **4-ring (4 motors)** or **6-ring (6 motors)**, ticking a mixed-cluster box (**mixed 2+2**, **mixed 3+3** or **mixed 4+2 / 2+2+2**, whichever that layout offers) adds rows that fly a mix of candidates. Its **aero model** starts on **Auto (recommended)** whatever Preferences flies, so match the two before you compare a row with a **Launch**. Batch is unavailable on a staged rocket or one carrying a **Booster (parallel stage)**. A passing row compares motors under your inputs — fit, launch conditions, recovery settings and model — rather than clearing a motor to fly.

**Black powder motor** — A motor whose propellant is black powder. The ejection charge of the stage below can light a black powder upper-stage motor directly; a composite motor generally will not light that way, whatever its size. *In the app:* the motor browser's **Propellant** chips (under **▸ All filters**) show what the catalogue records. When you pick a motor for any stage above the launch stage, the app sets its **Ignition** by propellant. A motor recorded as black powder starts on **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)**, lit by the stage below. Every other motor starts on **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)** with a 1 s delay: composites, hybrids, any motor whose propellant the catalogue leaves blank, and every motor you import from a `.eng` or `.rse` file (neither format records propellant). A strap-on booster always starts on Automatic. The motor's impulse letter and power class play no part. Picking a motor resets its mount's trigger, so choose the motor first, then set the trigger to match your hardware.

**Boattail** — A section that narrows toward the rocket's tail. It shrinks the flat base and so cuts base drag, but its own lift pushes the opposite way to the nose's, moving the CP forward and lowering your stability margin; the net effect depends on its shape and speed. *In the app:* build one from a **Transition** whose **Aft diameter** is smaller than its **Fore diameter** (a RASAero `.CDX1` boattail opens as a Transition named **Boat tail**). A boattail at least three times as long as the diameter it loses is charged no pressure drag of its own, except under the supersonic model (**Supersonic**, or **Auto** on a flight that passes Mach 0.9), which adds its wave drag above Mach 0.9.

**Body lift / Galejs correction** — The normal force a rocket's body makes when it meets the air at an angle, beyond what the original Barrowman terms give for changes in diameter. Its effect on the combined CP depends on where it acts relative to the other components' forces. *In the app:* it is always on, under every aerodynamics model; it acts at the center of the body's side profile and grows with the angle of attack. Your Design workspace CP is worked out at zero angle of attack, where body lift is zero, so it shows only in flight — one reason the report's **CP at launch guide exit** can sit forward of the Design CP in a crosswind.

**Body tube** — A main tubular section of the airframe. Its diameter, length, wall thickness, material and surface finish all feed your rocket's mass and drag. *In the app:* a **Body tube** takes **Length**, **Outer diameter** (**Outer radius** when Preferences → Display → **Round components entered as** is **Radius**), **Wall thickness** or the same dimension as **Inner diameter**, **Material** and **Surface finish**. In a minimum-diameter rocket the body tube is the motor mount: tick **Motor mount (motor loads in this tube)**. See **Minimum-diameter / sub-minimum rocket**.

**Booster / sustainer** — A booster powers an earlier part of a staged flight and then drops away; the sustainer is the top stage, which flies on after the lower stages separate. A booster can sit below the sustainer in a serial stack or beside it as a strap-on. *In the app:* a new design's stage is named **Sustainer**; **+ Add stage** adds a stage named **Booster** below it, and a strap-on is the **Booster (parallel stage)** assembly. Each separated booster flies its own branch (a ring of strap-ons flies as one) and gets its own line in the launch report, with its own apogee and landing rate, judged against the same 20 ft/s (6.1 m/s) target as the sustainer whether it tumbles or hangs under a canopy. Size any booster canopy from the booster's own weight. See **Stage / serial staging** and **Parallel booster / strap-on booster**.

**Boundary layer** — The thin layer of air next to a surface, slowed by viscosity relative to the flow outside it. Its behavior sets skin-friction drag and where the flow separates. *In the app:* wherever skin friction is worked out — every flight and the **Drag analysis** panel, under every aerodynamics model — the boundary layer is treated as fully turbulent, and there is no laminar setting. A part's **Surface finish** sets a roughness floor under its friction coefficient — the part pays the higher of the two — so a smoother finish lowers your drag only down to the turbulent value and never switches the app to laminar flow. See **Laminar / turbulent flow** and **Skin-friction drag**.

**Build allowance** — The mass component the **Measured mass & CG** box adds so your motorless model matches what the finished airframe weighs and where it balances. It stands for what the parts list leaves out, such as glue and paint. *In the app:* in that box, under the component tree on Design, type **Measured mass** and **Measured CG from nose tip** with the motor out and press **Add “Build allowance”**, which puts the mass at the distance from the nose tip the box names; after a re-weigh, **Update “Build allowance”** gives the same component its new mass and position rather than adding another. It is separate from the motor hardware the app infers from **Weighed pad mass with this motor**, which you weigh with the motor in. See **Weighed pad mass** and **Hardware mass**.

**Bulkhead** — A solid disk that closes off or divides a tube. Unlike a centering ring, it has no hole through the middle for another tube. *In the app:* a **Bulkhead** you add inside a body tube or tube coupler sizes itself to fill that tube (one opened from a `.ork` or `.rkt` file keeps any diameter the file states); you set its **Thickness (axial)** and material. It also stops a motor: **⌾ Estimate** beside a stage's **Max motor length** measures forward from the mount's aft end to the first bulkhead or engine block — usually your av-bay's aft bulkhead — so model that bulkhead for a real figure. See **Max motor length**.

**Burnout mass** — The mass left once the propellant has burned. For a motor that is the spent casing and whatever hardware stays with it; for a rocket it also depends on which stages are still attached, so it differs from your motorless dry mass. *In the app:* the Design workspace's **Recovery weight** tile, beside **Mass (loaded)** in **▤ All stats**, is worked out from your design: the dry rocket (on a staged design, the sustainer and stages that stay attached) plus its retained motors at burnout mass, or loaded mass when **Ignition** is **Never**. The **Recovery sizing** panel uses that figure. The report's **Recovery weight (after burnout)** is read from the flight instead, once the last burnout, the last separation and the first deployment are past; add it to Results as a **Recovery weight** tile with **⚙ Choose metrics**. On a staged design, or one with a separating strap-on, the two can differ. See **Recovery mass / recovery weight** and **Dry mass / empty mass**.

**Burn time / burnout** — Burn time is how long a motor produces thrust; burnout is the moment that burn ends. A catalogue's rated burn time may use a defined thrust cutoff and so differ from the last timestamp in a simulator file. [ThrustCurve: motor statistics and burn-time normalization](https://www.thrustcurve.org/info/motorstats.html). *In the app:* the motor browser's **Burn (s)** column shows the catalogue's rated figure. A flight burns out when the thrust curve it flies runs out, so the report's **Time to burnout** follows the curve file rather than the catalogue. A motor you import from a `.eng` or `.rse` file takes its **Burn (s)** from its own last timestamp. See **Thrust curve**.

### C

**Caliber, cal** — One body diameter, used as the unit of stability margin: with the CG 100 mm ahead of the CP on a 50 mm rocket, your margin is 2 calibers. *In the app:* in **▤ All stats**, **Stability** always reads in calibers of the rocket's **Max diameter**, beside a **Stability (of length)** tile in percent. The vitals strip, the stats readout on the Design canvas, the 2D and 3D callouts, the Fly screen and the margins on **Results** follow Preferences → Display → **Stability shown as**: **Calibers** (the default), **% of length** or **Both**. Calibers scale the CG–CP gap to the rocket's width, so the same 2 calibers is about 10 % of the length on a rocket 20 diameters long and about 25 % on one 8 diameters long. The **Stability margin** flight plot and the launch report's under- or over-stable warning stay in calibers whatever you choose.

**Camera shroud / fairing** — A housing that stands off the airframe around an external camera or a similar item. It moves the CP as well as adding drag. *In the app:* a **Camera shroud / fairing** on a body tube, sized by length, width and height, with separate **Fore end (toward the nose)** and **Aft end (toward the tail)** shapes. The app flies its side outline as a low, slender lifting surface and charges drag on its frontal area measured from the tube surface; the app scales the panel's nominal figure by the body's drag relative to its **Classic Extended Barrowman** drag at Mach 0.3, so your selected model can change it even at Mach 0.3. If that reference cannot be measured, the nominal figure stays fixed. A file that models a shroud as a one-fin freeform set opens with an offer to **Convert to camera shroud**. Type the shroud's weight in **Mass (as built)**: a figure typed in its **Overrides (blank = calculated)** block does not reach the flight. See **Conformal** and **Protuberance**.

**Canopy** — The fabric body of a parachute. Its diameter, drag coefficient and spill hole, with the weight it carries, set how fast your rocket comes down; a diameter on its own says little about performance. *In the app:* a parachute's **Canopy diameter** is its nominal diameter, **Drag coefficient (blank = auto)** flies 0.80 when left blank, and a **Spill hole ⌀ (0 = none)** multiplies the Cd by 1 − (hole ⌀ ÷ canopy ⌀)², taking the vent's area off the canopy; the launch report lists the Cd each device flew. A rated Cd holds only with the area it was quoted against. Fruity Chutes quote theirs against the canopy minus its vent, the area the app flies, so enter their Cd and their spill hole together, or pick the canopy from **📦 Choose from preset database…**, which fills both. For a Cd quoted against the full canopy, leave the spill hole at 0; otherwise the vent comes off twice and the app shows a faster descent than the canopy gives.

**Cant angle** — How far a fin is tilted away from lining up with the rocket's axis. Cant makes the rocket roll as air flows over the fins. *In the app:* **Cant angle** on a fin set, in degrees; a new fin set reads *default: 0*. **Max roll rate** under **Show all details** on **Results** shows how fast a canted rocket spun. To turn the fins to a different position around the body instead, use **Rotation (about body axis)**.

**Case / casing / closure** — The motor's pressure-holding tube and the end hardware that seals it. A reloadable motor reuses a specified case and closures around a fresh reload; a single-use motor comes as one piece for one firing. The spent case and closure stay in the rocket after burnout, so your parachute lowers them. *In the app:* the **Motor case** row under **Show all details** names the hardware the catalogue lists, such as RMS-18/20, since the hardware is part of which motor it is, as much as the impulse letter. **Recovery weight** includes the spent casing, and **Weighed pad mass with this motor** on **Motors & Launch** carries the adapter, retainer and closure the catalogue weight leaves out. See **Reload / single-use motor**.

**Cd / CD / drag coefficient** — A dimensionless number that links drag to air density, airspeed and a stated reference area: `D = ½ × ρ × V² × Cd × A_ref`. It changes with Mach number, Reynolds number, surface finish and angle of attack. A Cd is a ratio rather than a force, and two Cd values compare only when their reference areas and conditions match. *In the app:* your whole rocket's Cd at Mach 0.3, referenced to its largest cross-section, is the **Cd (M0.3)** tile in **▤ All stats**; **Drag analysis** on **Results** plots it against Mach, and the flight-data download carries it at every step. A parachute or streamer takes its own in **Drag coefficient (blank = auto)**. See **Cd override** and **Reference area, A_ref / Ar**. [NASA: drag coefficient](https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-coefficient/).

**Cd override** — A drag coefficient you type in place of the one the app works out from a part's shape. *In the app:* **Drag coefficient (Cd)** in a part's **Overrides (blank = calculated)** block; blank reads *auto*, the computed drag. On an outside part (a nose cone, body tube, transition, fin set, tube fins, launch lug or rail button), your figure replaces that part's drag; tick **Use instead of everything inside** and it replaces the drag of the part and everything in it. A figure typed on a part inside the airframe, or on a camera shroud, never reaches the flight (see **Camera shroud / fairing**). A stage, pod set or booster has no drag of its own, so left unticked your figure adds to what its parts compute: a rocket that computed 0.60 flies at 1.60 with 1.0 typed unticked on its stage. The figure is charged once per copy: a fin set's field reads **Drag coefficient (Cd) — per fin**, so 0.5 on three fins adds 1.5, and a ring of three boosters ticked at 0.50 adds 1.50. The app charges your number unchanged at every speed, where drag worked out from the shape climbs toward Mach 1 and falls away above it. A Cd trimmed to match one flight fits the speeds that flight reached; a bigger motor keeps the number while taking the rocket through speeds where it fits poorly, so re-trim when your flight regime moves.

**Centering ring** — A flat ring that holds an inner tube, usually the motor mount, centered inside an outer tube. Its center is open, where a bulkhead is solid. *In the app:* a **Centering ring** inside a body tube or tube coupler takes a **Thickness (axial)** and a **Material**; the app sizes it to fill the gap between that tube's bore and the inner tube beside it, unless the file you opened states its own diameters. It adds mass and moves the CG like any visible part. Place it along the inner tube: a ring with no inner tube alongside it has no hole and weighs as a solid disc. **🖨 STL for printing (mm)** and **✂ DXF (CNC/laser, 1:1)** cut the bore your file states, else the outside diameter of the first inner tube in the tube that holds the ring, whether or not the ring sits alongside it, with no warning when a file's bore is smaller than that motor tube, so check an imported ring's bore before you print or cut it. With no inner tube in that tube at all, they cut a bore half the ring's outside diameter and label the part *assumed bore*, while the flight weighs the ring as a solid disc.

**Center of gravity, CG** — The rocket's balance point: where its whole mass acts as if it sat in one place. It moves as the propellant burns (usually forward, since the motor sits in the tail) and jumps when a stage separates. *In the app:* every whole-rocket CG is measured from the nose tip toward the tail: **CG (empty)** and **CG (loaded)** in **▤ All stats**, **CG** in the stats readout on the Design canvas, and the **CG location** flight plot on **Results**. **CG from component top** in a part's **Overrides (blank = calculated)** block pins one part's CG, and the **Measured mass & CG** box matches the model to the rocket you weighed and balanced. Each motor's own CG stays at the middle of its length through the burn; the app ignores any CG trace an imported `.rse` carries.

**Center of pressure, CP** — The point along the rocket where the aerodynamic side force acts: the force that, with the CP behind the CG, turns a disturbed rocket back into line. It depends on the shape and on flight conditions, Mach number and angle of attack among them. You find it by calculation, where the CG is a point you can find by balancing, and it is a different point from where the drag acts. *In the app:* **CP** in **▤ All stats** and in the stats readout is measured from the nose tip at Mach 0.3 and zero angle of attack, under the aerodynamics model you have selected. It is the most-forward CP over a full sweep of roll angles, so clocking the fins or a shroud leaves it where it is; where clocking matters (fewer than three fins, or a part off the axis), a **CP in this plane** tile shows the CP for the parts as they sit. A design with nothing yet making lift shows a dash. In flight the CP moves with Mach number and angle of attack, and again when a stage separates: see the **CP location** flight plot, **CP at launch guide exit** under **Show all details**, and the CP-vs-Mach chart in **Drag analysis**.

**CFD — computational fluid dynamics** — Computer simulation of the airflow around a shape, solving the flow equations over a fine mesh. It can estimate pressures, wakes and aerodynamic loads in far more detail than a component-by-component engineering model. *In the app:* the app computes with engineering models (the Barrowman method and its extensions) and runs no CFD solver. The guide quotes published CFD results where they bear on a limit, such as how far a camera shroud can move the CP, and notes that the CFD work puts no number on the wake a shroud sheds onto the fins behind it (*Designing the Rocket → Camera shrouds (fairings) — how they're modeled*). CFD results are predictions too; wind-tunnel and flight measurements are what both are checked against.

**Chord / root chord / tip chord** — A fin's front-to-back length. The *root chord* runs along the body where the fin attaches; the *tip chord* is its outer edge. *In the app:* **Root chord** and **Tip chord** on trapezoidal fins, **Root chord** alone on elliptical fins; a freeform fin's root chord is its outline's root edge, where the outline returns to the body. The app positions your fin set by its root chord even where a swept tip reaches farther aft than the root's trailing end, and **📐 Fin template (SVG, 1:1)**, **🖨 STL for printing (mm)** and **✂ DXF (CNC/laser, 1:1)** all measure that same root chord and place the tab along it.

**Classic Extended Barrowman** — The aerodynamics setting that flies the OpenRocket 24.12 kernel's own aerodynamics, with the lift and drag terms the app adds switched off, apart from the few behaviors listed at the end of this entry. *In the app:* **Classic Extended Barrowman** under Preferences → Aerodynamics → **Aerodynamics model**, or in the vitals strip's **Aero** selector for this session only. It turns off the Kbf body carryover, the sharp-airfoil fin pressure model, any named **Supersonic airfoil** section (with its **LE chamfer length**, **TE chamfer length** and **LE bluntness radius** drag), the ×1.8 fin interference factor on fin skin friction, the subsonic pressure-drag floor on a stubby nose cone and the supersonic model, and it ignores a stage's **Nozzle exit diameter**, so neither power-on base drag nor pressure thrust flies. Use it to compare with desktop OpenRocket, and expect a particular desktop run to differ for other reasons too: the app seeds turbulence from a fixed number, 42, so your re-run repeats exactly, where desktop OpenRocket draws a fresh seed for each simulation; and different inputs, motor curves or simulation settings change results. A few behaviors sit outside the switch: a mass override with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked scales that part's moments of inertia with its mass; an inner tube off the centerline, or a spread cluster, adds roll inertia that 24.12 leaves out, so **Roll inertia (loaded)** in **▤ All stats** reads higher here than desktop OpenRocket gives; and a streamlined protuberance's or camera shroud's drag follows the body's drag with Mach.

**Cluster** — Two or more motors side by side that fire together. *In the app:* an inner tube's **Cluster layout** turns one tube into a pattern (**Single**, pairs, rows, rings, stars and a nine-tube grid, each option naming its count), and one motor choice fills every tube: thrust multiplies by the count and each motor's mass sits at its own tube. **Cluster spacing (× tube ⌀)** sets how far apart the tubes sit (at 1, neighboring tubes touch; the two nine-tube patterns spread wider), and **Cluster rotation** turns the whole pattern. At 0 the pattern sits as desktop OpenRocket lays it out, which leaves the 3- to 6-ring and 3- to 6-star patterns with no tube straight up (a star's center tube sits on the axis, and a 3-ring's odd tube sits to the right), so check the **Aft** view; see **Aft view**. The mount's card on **Motors & Launch** says how many motors one pick fires, such as *cluster ×4*, or *×3 — one per pod* inside a pod set. If you want different motors side by side, give each its own inner tube placed with **Distance off centerline**; on a 4-ring or 6-ring, **Batch simulate motors…** can also fly two motor types in symmetric halves (**mixed 2+2**, **mixed 3+3**), and a 6-ring as three opposite pairs (**mixed 4+2 / 2+2+2**). The stage's **Nozzle exit diameter** is one equivalent nozzle whose exit area is the sum of every motor's; the app fills it in only when every motor firing in that stage has a published exit. If any one lacks one, the app fills nothing and clears a figure the previous motor left, so type the equivalent exit yourself; a blank field flies neither the power-on drag reduction nor pressure thrust.

**Coast** — The unpowered part of a flight after a motor burns out, when only gravity and aerodynamic forces act and the rocket slows toward apogee. A staged rocket can also coast between one stage's burnout and the next stage's ignition. *In the app:* on a single-stage flight your coast runs from **Time to burnout** to **Time to apogee** under **Show all details**, and the **Optimal delay** line on **Results** is the simulator's coast from burnout to apogee, with the recommended delay beside it rounded to a whole second. That coast is worked out as if nothing opened early, so when your chute came out before apogee it runs longer than the gap between those two rows. **Drag analysis** plots the power-off curve your rocket flies while coasting.

**Component / part / component tree** — One modeled item of the rocket, and the hierarchy that shows what sits inside what: a body tube can hold a motor mount, recovery gear and other parts, and carry fins on its outside. The guide calls an item a *component* or a *part*, interchangeably. *In the app:* the tree is the left column of the **Design** workspace. A selected part's row offers **↑** and **↓** (reorder inside its parent), **⧉** (Duplicate), **⎘** (Copy), **✂** (Cut) and **✕** (Delete); a stage has no Copy or Cut, and a rocket's only stage has no Delete. A copied or cut part goes back in through the **⎗ Paste into …** buttons under the tree. Every one of these takes the part's contents with it, a body tube with its fins and motor mount as much as a pod set or booster, with one exception: a duplicated or pasted motor mount arrives empty, because its motor stays with the original, so load one on **Motors & Launch**.

**Component preset** — A catalogue entry for a real part, with its maker, part number, dimensions and material. *In the app:* select a part and press **📦 Choose from preset database…**, on the eleven part types that have a catalogue (inner tubes share the body-tube list). A pick writes what the row records: dimensions, material, a nose cone's or transition's shape (its shape parameter back at that shape's default) and whether it is solid, a row that does not say solid making it hollow. A parachute takes the row's whole set (diameter, Cd, spill hole, shroud lines, materials) and a streamer its Cd and material, with anything the row lacks back at the default, so a canopy with no rated Cd flies 0.80 rather than the last canopy's rating; a rigid part keeps any dimension or material the row leaves out. Where the row records a mass (most parachutes do, most tubes and nose cones do not), it becomes the part's **Mass** override and stays through later dimension edits until you clear that field. A row with no mass clears the previous catalogue part's mass, keeps a mass you typed, and returns a parachute to its computed mass; a weight you entered with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked stays whatever you pick. Without an override, mass comes from geometry and material density. A row holding a number a field cannot take is brought inside its limits, with a note under the button.

**Composite motor** — A motor whose grain is a composite propellant, usually ammonium perchlorate composite propellant (APCP). A composite grain generally will not light from the ejection charge of the stage below, so in a staged rocket it needs its own igniter, usually fired by electronics. *In the app:* on a staged design each motor's card on **Motors & Launch** has an **Ignition** setting. A motor loaded into any stage above the launch stage starts on **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)** with a 1 s delay, unless the catalogue records its propellant as black powder, which starts on **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)**. A motor with no recorded propellant, every imported `.eng` or `.rse` motor among them, also starts electronics-timed, and a strap-on booster starts on **Automatic** whatever it burns, because it lights at launch. The line is the propellant, whatever the power class: plenty of low- and mid-power composites need electronics too. Treat these as starting points and set the trigger to match your staging hardware after you pick the motor, since picking a motor resets that mount's trigger.

**Compressibility** — How much the air's density changes as the flow around the rocket squeezes it. It barely matters at low speed and grows important as the flow nears and passes the speed of sound, which is why one low-speed Cd cannot describe a transonic or supersonic flight. *In the app:* no readout names it, but you can see its effect: **Drag analysis** on **Results** shows your drag rising from about Mach 0.9 toward its transonic peak, while the **Cd (M0.3)** tile is a low-speed figure and a typed **Cd override** stays flat throughout. For a fast flight, trust the computed curve over any single number.

**Conformal** — Shaped to fit the curve of the surface it sits on. A conformal camera-shroud underside follows the curve of the body tube; a flat one sits on the tangent line and leaves gaps at its corners. *In the app:* **Conformal to body tube?** on a camera shroud, ticked by default. It changes the drawings (the **Aft** and **3D** views) and the whole-rocket 3D exports (**Export .obj — 3D geometry**, **Export .glb — 3D model with colors**, **Export .stl — 3D shell (reference)**), and leaves your drag, mass, CG and CP the same either way. The drag is charged on a frontal area measured from the tube surface, so the gap under a flat base counts as blocked flow just as the material filling it on a conformal shroud does; the mass is the **Mass (as built)** you enter; and the lifting outline is the same with or without the tick.

**Coriolis acceleration** — The apparent sideways push that comes from describing motion on a rotating Earth; it grows with speed and depends on latitude. It is separate from aerodynamic steering, wind drift and the rocket's own roll. *In the app:* the simulator adds it on a spherical Earth from your **Latitude (°)** in **Launch conditions**; **Longitude (°)** leaves it unchanged. The flight-data download carries it as the **Ac — Coriolis acceleration** column, with a chart tab of its own in **⬇ Flight data + charts (.xlsx)**. On a hobby flight it is tiny next to gravity and thrust.

**CP travel** — How far the CP moves along the rocket as the Mach number changes; on a fast flight it can eat into your stability margin. *In the app:* the **Drag analysis** panel's **Center of pressure vs Mach** chart draws it. Under **Supersonic**, or **Auto** once it has switched, the chart follows the body's CP past Mach 1; the two Barrowman models hold the body's CP fixed above Mach 1, and the panel says so. Before a fast flight, compare the CP at your **Max Mach** with your CG. See **Center of pressure, CP**.

**Cross-section** — The shape you would see if you cut straight through an object. For a tube it is a circle or a ring (annulus); for a fin it is the thickness profile, seen cutting across the fin from leading edge to trailing edge. *In the app:* a fin set's **Cross section** offers **Square**, **Rounded** and **Airfoil (pointed)**, and the choice changes the fin's drag and its computed weight: the app counts a **Rounded** fin at 99 % and an **Airfoil (pointed)** fin at 85 % of the material a **Square** fin of the same outline and thickness holds, with any through-the-wall tab and fillet counted in full. The **Aft** view shows your whole rocket end-on. See **Airfoil / fin cross-section** for what each fin choice does, including the supersonic sections.

### D–E

**Damping** — The aerodynamic resistance to turning that grows with how fast the rocket turns, so a wobble dies away. Static stability sets the restoring push after a gust; damping decides how quickly the swinging settles. *In the app:* the simulator applies pitch, yaw and roll damping moments, and the **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** download carries **Cζθ — Pitch damping coefficient** and **CζΦ — Roll damping coefficient** columns. A positive stability margin tells you the rocket turns back toward the wind; the **Angle of attack** flight plot shows how fast your rocket settles. See **Stability margin / static margin**.

**Delay** — A wait, in seconds, counted from a particular event. *In the app:* **Ejection delay (s)** counts from the motor's burnout; the number beside a staged motor's **Ignition** choice counts from the trigger picked there; **Deploy delay** counts from a recovery device's **Deploy at** trigger; and **Separation delay** counts from the separation trigger: **Separate at (lower stages)** on a stage, **Separate at** on a **Booster (parallel stage)**. Check the event as well as the number: two equal delays counted from different events fire at different times. See **Ignition event / ignition delay** and **Separation event / separation delay**.

**Density altitude** — The altitude at which a standard day has air as thin as the air you are describing. It measures how thin the air is, not where the pad sits. *In the app:* a readout in the **Launch conditions** panel on **Motors & Launch**, beside **Time step (s)**, worked out from the **Temperature** and **Station pressure** your flight uses, typed or filled in from **Site altitude**. With both boxes blank it equals your site altitude, grayed; a hot day or a low barometer raises it — 7,122 ft at a 4,000 ft field on a 95 °F day. Each run stores it: the launch report shows it under **Show all details**, and **⬇ Run table (.csv)** and **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)** carry it as their last column. It is a readout only, so type your pad's real elevation in **Site altitude** and the day's air in **Temperature** and **Station pressure** — never a density altitude. It is dry-air density altitude, so a muggy day's true figure is higher (about 400 ft at 50 % humidity at that field), and your flight still flies the pad's real temperature and pressure, which set the speed of sound and a motor's pressure thrust. [NWS: density altitude](https://www.weather.gov/zse/HighDensityAltitude).

**Deployment / deployment trigger** — Deployment is the release of a recovery device; the trigger is the event that releases it. *In the app:* each parachute and streamer has **Deploy at** — **Motor ejection charge** (a motor's charge in the same stage), **Apogee**, **Altitude (descending)** at the height in **Deploy altitude (AGL)**, **Launch** or **Never** — plus a **Deploy delay**. Electronics are modeled by the trigger they fire on: an altimeter by **Apogee** or an altitude, a timer by **Launch** plus a delay. The trigger sets the timing only; whether your electronics and charge work is outside the simulation. Behind a plugged motor, a device on **Motor ejection charge** never opens. See **Dual deployment**.

**Deployment velocity / opening speed** — How fast a recovery device is moving when it opens. *In the app:* the launch report's recovery table gives it in the **Opens at** column, one row per device, and the saved-runs spreadsheet's **Velocity at deployment** column carries the first device's figure. The first device reads the rocket's speed over the ground; a device that opens while the rocket already hangs under an earlier canopy reads the descent rate, so wind drift is kept out of it. Above 70 ft/s the device's verdict reads **hard opening**; a line in the report calls 70 to 90 ft/s a caution and anything faster a warning, and **Safe deployment** reads yes only at 70 ft/s or less. The simulator's own note flags any opening over 20 m/s (65.6 ft/s), a stricter line. These check speed only: shock-cord force and zippering in a particular tube are outside what the app calculates. See **Opening shock**.

**Descent rate** — How fast the rocket comes down, counted vertically and given as a positive number; speed over the ground adds the sideways drift. *In the app:* the recovery table shows **Descent after** (vertical) beside **Over ground**, and the report's **Landing descent rate**, its **Landing rate OK (≤ 20 ft/s)** check and the Fly screen's **Descent** all use the vertical figure. Judge your canopy on that figure; the ground speed is what your rocket lands at in wind. See **Landing rate**.

**Deterministic simulation** — A calculation that repeats exactly when its inputs, model and engine stay the same. *In the app:* the simulated gusts and the tiny pitch and yaw perturbations use one fixed seed, 42, so **Launch** on an unchanged design gives you the identical flight, and **📈 Show charts** re-flies a saved run exactly; desktop OpenRocket draws a fresh seed for each simulation. So repeating **Launch** shows you one flight, never the scatter that real gusts and motor-to-motor differences give real flights: a **Wind gusts σ** gives one plausible gusty flight rather than a spread of landings. See **Wind avg / Wind gusts σ**.

**Diameter class** — The nominal size a motor casing is sold in — 6, 13, 18, 24, 29, 38, 54, 75/76, 98 mm and up — rather than its exact diameter. *In the app:* the motor browser's **Diameter** chips (also in the **Batch simulate motors…** dialog) and the **⌀** size on each mount's card. A motor within 1.5 mm of a common size counts as that class, and one near none of them keeps its own diameter as its class, so the chips can also show sizes such as 10.5, 20, 32 and 64 mm; 75 and 76 mm share **75/76 mm**. A mount takes every class up to 1 mm over its bore, the smaller ones riding in an adapter. The app adds no adapter, so model yours or enter a weighed pad mass to carry its weight. See **Adapter** and *Motors → Diameter-class fit (adapter-down)*.

**Diameter / radius / bore** — A diameter spans a circle through its center; the radius is half of it. A tube's bore is its inside diameter, and its wall thickness is `(outside diameter − inside diameter) ÷ 2`. *In the app:* round sizes read as diameters while Preferences → Display → **Round components entered as** is on **Diameter**, the default. A tube's **Wall thickness** and **Inner diameter** boxes are two views of one number; edit either. The motor browser fits motors to the mount's bore. Keep the motor-case diameter, the mount bore and the stage's **Nozzle exit diameter** apart when you type. See *Designing the Rocket → Dimensions: diameter vs. radius*.

**Drag** — The aerodynamic force that resists the rocket's motion through the air. It grows with air density and the square of airspeed as well as with shape. *In the app:* the **Cd (M0.3)** stat tile gives your design's drag coefficient at Mach 0.3; the **Drag analysis** panel splits the coefficient **By type** into **Friction**, **Pressure / wave** and **Base**, or **By component**; the **Drag force** flight plot shows the force along one flight. A Cd chart describes the shape; the force your rocket meets depends on the flight. See **Cd / CD / drag coefficient**.

**Drag analysis / aerodynamic sweep** — The design's drag coefficient, its breakdown and its CP across a range of Mach numbers at zero angle of attack, computed without flying a trajectory. *In the app:* the **Drag analysis** panel on **Results**, opened with **Show CD vs Mach**. Its **Conditions** selector picks the air: **Sea level**, the default, is 101,325 Pa at 20 °C — the simulation kernel's own default rather than the 15 °C standard day; **At altitude…** uses the standard atmosphere at a height you type; **File Mach-Alt table** appears only after you open a RASAero .CDX1 that carries one. Your launch-site settings play no part. The solid **CD power-off** curve is the coast. Once any stage has a **Nozzle exit diameter**, a dashed **CD power-on** curve appears, assuming every stage's motor is burning and differing from power-off in base drag only; under **Classic Extended Barrowman** it lies exactly on the solid one. See *Visualizing the Design → Drag analysis (CD vs Mach)*.

**Drag class** — The category a protuberance's drag is worked out from. *In the app:* **Drag class** on a **Protuberance (drag bump)**. **Streamlined, no base (raceway, cable tunnel) — Cd = body CD without base drag** and **Streamlined, blunt back (camera housing, shoe) — Cd = body CD with base drag**, the default, charge the bump your own body's drag coefficient per unit of frontal area; **Inclined flat plate (fin bracket, anchor) — Cd = 1.17·sin²θ** takes θ from **Plate angle from the body**. A Cd you type in **Cd on frontal area (blank or 0 = from class)** replaces the class. A camera shroud's drag comes from its **Fore end (toward the nose)** and **Aft end (toward the tail)** shapes instead. See **Protuberance** and **Camera shroud / fairing**.

**Drift / landing drift** — Sideways travel from the pad, mostly under recovery. *In the app:* with **Show all details** open, the report gives **Landing distance from pad** (the horizontal distance from the launch point to touchdown; on a multistage rocket, the sustainer's) and a **Landing bearing**. The wind is one average speed plus gusts, the same at every height and always from the east, so a windy flight typically drifts west and **Landing bearing** reads near 270° (W) with "downwind" beside it. At your field, treat 270° as downwind along the day's real wind and keep the displayed bearing's offset from it; **Rod aim** can also put the landing across or upwind. **☁ Get weather…** can fill **Wind avg** with one hour's forecast (or ERA5 reanalysis) wind at 10 m (33 ft); the forecast's direction is shown for reference only. **Rod aim** moves drift too: a rod leaned downwind drifts further, and one aimed across the wind lands the rocket off to the side it leans. An imported multilevel wind profile is replaced by the file's average-wind settings, and the ground is flat at the pad's altitude. See **Rod aim**.

**Drill-to-fit** — Shortening an adjustable motor delay by drilling out delay grain, so the charge fires at the second you choose. *In the app:* the rule behind the launch report's **recommended (drill to fit)** delay: the simulated optimum rounded to the nearest whole second, never snapped to the delays the motor is sold with. **auto (optimal)** on the top motor flies it; **Ejection delay (s)**, or **Custom (drilled)…** in the motor browser, flies your own. Drilling only shortens a delay, so when the recommendation is longer than the longest delay listed after **prescribed:**, fly that longest one or deploy with electronics. See **Ejection delay** and *Motors → Delays and the drill-to-fit rule*.

**Drogue** — The smaller recovery device that opens first in a dual-deploy descent, usually at apogee. It slows and steadies the airframe while still falling faster than the main would, which limits drift from high up. *In the app:* any device that opens before the last one is judged as a drogue — preferred to 70 ft/s, a caution to 90 ft/s, a warning above, flagged **drogue descent too fast** — and the **Recovery sizing** panel sizes drogues for 60 ft/s. The optional **Drogue descent** tile on **Results** (add it with **⚙ Choose metrics**) shows the descent rate the report records under the first device that is not your landing one. It stays out when the main opens. A drogue in the caution band makes your main open in that band too, because the main opens while the rocket is still falling at the drogue's rate. See **Dual deployment**.

**Dry mass / empty mass** — The rocket's mass with no motor installed, with the recovery gear and payload in the design included. It is not the mass after burnout: a spent motor case still weighs something. *In the app:* the **Mass (empty)** stat tile, beside **Mass (loaded)**, with **CG (empty)** for its balance point. Other sources sometimes use "dry mass" differently, so check what is included before you compare figures. See **Burnout mass**.

**Dual deployment** — A recovery sequence with two deployment events, usually a drogue near apogee and a main parachute lower on the way down. *In the app:* give one device **Apogee** and the other **Altitude (descending)**, with its height in **Deploy altitude (AGL)**. The first device stays out when the second opens, and your descent under the main is worked out from both canopies together. A separating booster can fly its own dual-deploy pair, with its own recovery table in the report. See **Drogue**.

**Dynamic pressure, q / max Q** — The pressure scale of the airflow, `q = ½ × ρ × V²`, using air-relative speed; max Q is its largest value in a flight. It is a different quantity from atmospheric pressure, and its peak can come before top speed, because the air thins with altitude. *In the app:* the app does not show this term — no plot, export or report carries q or max Q; it is defined here for reading outside material. [NASA: dynamic pressure in the drag equation](https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-coefficient/).

**Ejection charge** — A small charge that separates the airframe and deploys recovery: the motor's own, fired at burnout plus the ejection delay, or one fired by electronics. A plugged motor has none. *In the app:* **Motor ejection charge** (a recovery device's **Deploy at**) and **This stage's ejection charge** (a stage's separation trigger) always mean a motor's charge in that stage; the **plugged** box on a motor's card removes it. Model an electronic charge by its trigger: **Apogee**, **Altitude (descending)**, or **Launch** plus a delay for a timer; for separation, the burnout, ignition and altitude triggers. A lower stage's charge can light the motor above it through the **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)** or **Lower stage ejection charge + delay** ignition choices. That works for black powder and fails for composite, so when you load a motor on any stage above the launch stage, the app sets it to **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)** at 1 s unless the catalogue records it as black powder: composites, hybrids and motors with no propellant on record, every imported motor among them, start there. A design you open keeps the ignition saved in it, so an upper-stage composite motor saved on **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)** flies as if the charge below lit it — a sustainer burn your hardware cannot produce; set each upper-stage motor's **Ignition** to match how you will really light it. The app models when a charge fires; its gas pressure, its sizing and the mechanical deployment are yours to check.

**Ejection delay** — The time from a motor's burnout to its ejection charge. *In the app:* **Ejection delay (s)** on the motor's card, with the delays the motor is sold with listed after **prescribed:**. The launch report gives three figures. **Optimal delay** is the simulated coast from the last motor burnout to apogee, as if no recovery device had opened early. **recommended (drill to fit)** is that optimum rounded to the nearest whole second, rather than snapped to the printed delays, because the app assumes you drill an adjustable delay to the second you want. **flown with** is what that run used: the recommended delay when **auto (optimal)** is ticked on the top motor, otherwise what you entered. Any other mount whose motor is on Auto — picked as **Auto (optimal)** in the motor browser, loaded that way from a RockSim file, or ticked while it was the top motor — shows **auto — top motor only** instead: it flies the delay in its field, and you can untick it. See **Drill-to-fit** and **Motor designation / common name**.

**Engine block / thrust ring** — A ring inside the motor mount that the motor bears against, so thrust drives the airframe rather than pushing the motor forward. It can also limit the usable motor length. Aft retention is separate: it keeps the motor from leaving the mount the other way. *In the app:* an **Engine block** inside an inner tube or body tube, with **Length** and **Wall thickness**; it adds mass, and **⌾ Estimate** for **Max motor length** measures forward to it. The motor's position comes from the mount's aft end and **Motor overhang (past aft end)**, so moving the block leaves your motor where it is.

**Equivalent nozzle exit diameter** — The diameter of one circle with the same area as several nozzle exits combined: `d_equivalent = √(d₁² + d₂² + …)`, or `d × √N` for N identical exits, so four 10 mm exits equal one 20 mm exit. Adding the diameters would give the wrong area. *In the app:* a stage has one **Nozzle exit diameter**, so a cluster or a set of pods takes the equivalent. When every motor in the stage has a published exit and the field is empty, the app fills in the equivalent, pods counted, and the line under the field says the areas were summed; if any motor lacks one, the field is left for you to fill. A value you typed, or one your file brought in, is kept, and a caution under the field gives the published figure when the two differ; swap the stage's motors and the value follows the new ones. See **Nozzle exit diameter**.

**ERA5 reanalysis** — ECMWF's reconstruction of past weather: a weather model re-run over the historical record with each day's observations folded in, hourly, back to 1940. *In the app:* **☁ Get weather…** answers any date more than 92 days back from Open-Meteo's archive and labels it **ERA5 reanalysis — the weather as it was, not a forecast**; the fields it sets say *reanalysis* where a forecast says *forecast*, and the gust button reads **Estimate from reanalysis gust**. Use it to re-fly a past launch in that day's air. From 2017 on, the archive answers from ECMWF's 9 km IFS model, still labeled ERA5 reanalysis. See **Get weather (☁ Get weather…)**.

**EX / custom motor** — A motor flown from a simulator file you import rather than picked from the bundled catalogue.
