"EX" usually means an experimental or research motor, but an imported file can describe a commercial motor too. *In the app:* **⬆ Import .eng/.rse** or **📁 Import EX folder** in the motor browser; the motors list under the maker **EX** with the file's manufacturer kept, stay in your browser between sessions, and fly like catalogue motors. An .rse stating a believable exit fills the stage's **Nozzle exit diameter**; for any other, type it yourself. Neither format records propellant, so in an upper stage the app starts an imported motor on electronics-timed ignition. The file supplies simulation data only; certification and flight eligibility are separate questions.

**Extrapolation** — Using a model beyond the conditions its supporting data covers. The equation still returns a number there, but nothing has checked it. *In the app:* the **Drag analysis** panel marks the limits: on the two Barrowman models, values above about Mach 1.5 are labeled approximate; on **Supersonic**, CP and drag are validated against NASA wind-tunnel data to about Mach 4.6, and the panel says to treat anything above about Mach 10 as extrapolation. A flight past Mach 0.9 on either Barrowman model gets a report line saying the model is approximate there. Read numbers from those regions as estimates, and fly with margin.

### F–H

**Fineness ratio, L/D** — Length divided by diameter: how slender a body or a nose is. A 5:1 nose is five times as long as its base is wide, and the whole rocket has a ratio of its own, so say which one you mean. *In the app:* no field shows it; you set a nose's fineness through its **Length** and **Base outer diameter**. Under Rogers Kbf, Auto and Supersonic, an ellipsoid, power, parabolic or Haack nose shorter than 1.8 times its base diameter pays extra subsonic pressure drag, and on every model the whole rocket's fineness sets a skin-friction correction.

**Fin set** — A group of identical fins sharing one set of dimensions and placement settings. *In the app:* **Trapezoidal fins**, **Elliptical fins**, **Freeform fins** or **Tube fins**. **Fin count** (1 to 8; if you type more, the set gets 8, the most the simulation flies) and **Rotation (about body axis)** arrange the fins around the body. The three flat kinds also have a **Cant angle**, which tilts each fin to the airflow so the rocket rolls rather than moving it around the body, and their planform — chords, sweep and height, or the drawn outline — with **Thickness** and **Cross section** sets each fin's shape. A tube-fin set is a ring of short tubes instead, set by **Length**, outer diameter and **Wall thickness**, with no cant or cross-section; leave its diameter blank and three or more tubes are sized to touch one another, while one or two take the body tube's own diameter.

**Fin tab / through-the-wall tab** — The part of a fin that passes through the body wall, often down to the motor mount tube. It adds mass and shapes the build, apart from the exposed outline the air sees. *In the app:* flat fin sets carry **Tab depth (0 = none)**, **Tab length**, **Tab offset** and **Tab offset from**; a tab exists only when depth and length are both above zero, and **Fit tab to motor tube** sets the depth to reach the motor tube (the body tube's wall thickness when it holds none) and, if the tab has no length yet, makes it 60 % of the root chord long. The tab's volume counts in the fin mass and CG, and the fin template, STL and DXF cut the same tab.

**Flight configuration** — A set of motor assignments, with the flight settings that go with them, carried inside one design; it may have a name. *In the app:* configurations arrive with a file or a share link: each configuration in a desktop OpenRocket `.ork`, and each motor-carrying simulation in a RockSim `.rkt` (those with the same motors fold into one) or a RASAero `.CDX1`. When a design carries two or more, the **Flight configurations** panel at the foot of Motors & Launch lists them by name, or by motor set when unnamed — every `.CDX1` one, a `.rkt` simulation RockSim named after its motors, and any `.ork` one never renamed in desktop OpenRocket. **Apply** switches its motors, ignition, recovery deployment, stage separation and the weighed pad mass entered for its motor, plus a `.CDX1`'s nozzle exit diameter; **None** unloads every motor. Your edits to those settings stay with the configuration on screen. The app has no control to create or rename one. An `.ork` save writes every configuration back, and gives a design with none a single unnamed one holding the loaded motors; a `.rkt` save keeps only the loaded motors, so save as `.ork` to keep them all. A configuration says what to fly; a saved run records a result (see **Saved run / run history**).

**Flight event** — Something that happens at one instant of a flight: ignition, leaving the launch guide, burnout, stage separation, apogee, deployment, landing. One event can trigger another action; altitude and velocity, which change all the time, are quantities instead. *In the app:* you choose events as triggers in a recovery device's **Deploy at**, a stage's **Separate at (lower stages)** and a staged motor's **Ignition**, and the launch report's **Show all details** gives their times (**Time to burnout**, **Time to apogee** and the rest), with each deployment's in the recovery table's **Deploys at** column.

**Flight time / time to apogee** — Flight time is the simulated time from launch to ground contact, or to the end of the recorded flight; time to apogee stops at the highest point. Neither is the motor's burn time or ejection delay. *In the app:* the Results tiles **Flight time** and **Apogee at**, the launch report's **Total flight time** and **Time to apogee**, and the run-table download's **Total flight time (s)** and **Time to apogee (s)** columns.

**Flown at Cd** — The drag coefficient a parachute or streamer really flew, which your descent rate and landing verdict rest on. *In the app:* a column of the launch report's recovery table. A canopy with no spill hole shows the coefficient you typed, or `0.80 (auto)` when you leave **Drag coefficient (blank = auto)** blank. A spill hole comes out of the coefficient, so a vented canopy shows both figures: `1.44 (1.50 less a 122 mm vent)` when you typed 1.50, and `0.77 (0.80 auto, less a 122 mm vent)` when you left it blank. A blank streamer reads —, since its value comes from the strip's length and material. A canopy left on auto flies 0.80, less any spill hole, whatever its rating, so type the manufacturer's figure before you trust the landing verdict.

**Fly screen** — The phone's home workspace, laid out for the flying field. *In the app:* the **Fly** tab, shown only in windows narrower than 768 px, where the app opens on it unless you were last working in another workspace. Beside your rocket and its stability it shows the latest flight's **Apogee**, **Optimum delay**, **Descent** and **Max velocity** (or those of a flight you pick in **Saved simulations**, marked **⚠ Flown with …** when the design, motor or conditions have changed since), the **Recovery weight**, a **Motor** row with **Change ▸**, the **Rod angle**, **Rod aim**, **Rod length** and **Wind avg** fields, **☁ Get weather…**, **⚖ Compare the motors in your range box ▸**, which opens Batch simulate when a motor is loaded on a design with one stage and no strap-ons, and **Launch**. See *Simulating and Reading Results → The Fly screen (phones, at the field)*.

**Freeform fin** — A flat fin whose outline you draw point by point instead of setting a trapezoid or an ellipse. “Freeform” names the planform only: the fin can still have rounded or airfoil edges and a through-the-wall tab. *In the app:* **Freeform fins** open a **Fin planform** editor, where you drag points or type their x and y. It needs at least three points, and an outline that crosses itself turns red and is refused. Freeform is the one fin type a transition takes, so fins on a transition arrive from a RockSim or RASAero file as freeform.

**Frontal area** — The area something presents to the oncoming air, seen head-on. For a bump on the airframe it is the bump's own area, separate from the rocket's reference area. *In the app:* you give a **Protuberance (drag bump)** its frontal area as **Width (across body)** × **Height (off the surface)** × **How many (identical)**, and its **Cd on frontal area (blank or 0 = from class)** is referenced to that area. The property panel shows the conversion: area times Cd, scaled to the reference area and added to the rocket's CD. A camera shroud shows the same line, its area measured from the tube surface.

**Get weather (☁ Get weather…)** — The button that fetches one hour's weather for one place from Open-Meteo and offers it to your launch conditions. *In the app:* beside the **Launch conditions** heading on Motors & Launch, and on the Fly screen. Pick a place, date and hour, and the review shows **Temperature**, **Station pressure**, **Wind avg**, **Latitude** and **Longitude** beside what the panel holds; **Apply** writes only the rows you leave ticked. The pressure is station pressure for your **Site altitude**, and when the terrain model's ground height differs from it by more than 30 m you choose which to fly. On Motors & Launch, a strip under the **Launch conditions** fields says where the weather came from and carries **Undo** and **Dismiss**. The gust and wind direction are shown, never applied — set **Rod aim** yourself. Dates more than 92 days back come labeled reanalysis (see **ERA5 reanalysis**). See *Launch Conditions → Getting the day's weather*.

**g / G (g-force)** — One standard g is 9.80665 m/s². In a physics equation, g usually means the local gravitational acceleration instead, which varies slightly with latitude and altitude. *In the app:* the unit is written **G**; pick it under Preferences → **Units of measure** → **Acceleration**. The run-table and batch downloads (.csv and .xlsx) carry an **Accel (Gs)** column, and **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** carries the local value as the series `g` (Gravitational acceleration). The app's acceleration — the Acceleration plot, **Max accel** and **Accel (Gs)** — is the size of the rocket's kinematic acceleration: thrust, drag and gravity together. An onboard accelerometer reads specific force instead, which is acceleration relative to free fall: about 1 g sitting on the pad, where the app shows zero; about 1 g more than the app during a near-vertical boost; about 1 g less during a vertical coast. Allow for that before you compare your altimeter's acceleration trace with the app's.

**Ground-relative speed / ground-hit velocity** — The size of the rocket's velocity relative to the ground, vertical and horizontal motion together: under a canopy, descent and wind drift combined. Elsewhere "groundspeed" often means horizontal speed only; in the app it always means the combined figure. *In the app:* the recovery table's **Over ground** column, the launch report's "… over the ground, the rest of that being wind drift" sentences, the optional **Ground speed at landing** tile (add it with **⚙ Choose metrics** on Results), and the **Ground hit velocity** column of the **⬇ Run table (.csv)** and **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)** downloads under **Saved simulations**, and of the Batch simulate dialog's **⬇ CSV** and **⬇ XLSX**. The landing check uses the vertical **Descent after** figure alone, so a windy day raises this number without failing the check. See **Descent rate**, **Airspeed / air-relative velocity** and **Velocity / vertical velocity**.

**Haack / Von Kármán** — A family of nose profiles derived from ideal low-wave-drag shapes: LD-Haack, usually called Von Kármán, has the least drag for a given length and base diameter, and LV-Haack for a given length and volume. *In the app:* you pick **Haack** as a nose cone's or transition's **Shape**; a **Shape parameter** of 0 (what a blank flies) is Von Kármán, 1/3 is LV-Haack, and the field stops at 1/3. The parameter changes the profile only, never the part's **Length** or base diameter.

**Hardware mass** — The extra, non-burning mass the app works out from your weighed pad mass: the weighed pad mass, minus the dry rocket, minus the loaded weight of every motor the rocket flies. It stands for what no catalogue counts — adapter, retainer, closure — without saying which. *In the app:* the line under **Weighed pad mass with this motor** (or **Weighed pad mass with every motor installed**) on Motors & Launch reads "… carried as hardware — adapter, retainer, closure", followed by the arithmetic behind it: weighed, minus dry, minus the catalogue motor weight. Each motor counts once per copy flown, clusters, pods and strap-ons included. The dry rocket is the airframe mass you entered under **Measured mass & CG**, or the app's own dry mass. The whole difference rides as inert mass on the motor in the primary mount, the topmost stage's (see **Primary mount**), split evenly across that mount's motors for the whole flight, so your recovery weight includes it. Nothing is carried when it comes out negative, when it outweighs the airframe, or after the motor set has changed since you weighed; a difference over half the motors' own weight flies but is flagged — check the entry.

**Hoerner** — Sighard Hoerner, whose book Fluid-Dynamic Drag collects measured drag coefficients for shapes, bumps and fittings. *In the app:* a **Camera shroud / fairing** starts from Hoerner's surface-protuberance values — 0.25 for **Streamlined (tapered)**, 0.55 for **Domed / half-round** and 1.05 for **Flat / blunt**, averaged over its ends, on its frontal area. Those are nominal coefficients: the app scales them in flight by your body's drag relative to its **Classic Extended Barrowman** drag at Mach 0.3, so your selected model can change them even at Mach 0.3. They stay fixed when that reference cannot be measured. **Inclined flat plate (fin bracket, anchor) — Cd = 1.17·sin²θ** also scales Hoerner's flat-plate value. The shroud has no wind-tunnel test of its own, so treat its drag as an estimate.

**Hybrid motor** — A motor whose fuel and oxidizer are in different phases, usually a solid fuel grain with a liquid oxidizer such as nitrous oxide, loaded separately. *In the app:* the launch report's **Motor type** row reads hybrid for one from the catalogue. As with every motor, the app flies its thrust curve and mass data and models no combustion. Assign a hybrid to a stage above the launch stage and its **Ignition** starts on **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)** with a 1 s delay, because the ejection charge of the stage below cannot light it. The app does the same for any motor whose propellant is not recorded as black powder, motors imported from .eng or .rse files included. A hybrid in the launch stage or on a strap-on starts on **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)**. That is only the starting setting: change it in the motor's **Ignition** on Motors & Launch.

**Hypersonic** — Conventionally, flight at about Mach 5 and above, where effects such as aerodynamic heating grow steadily more important. *In the app:* there is no hypersonic setting or warning. The Drag analysis panel's **Max Mach** runs to 5, and to 10 or 25 on the Supersonic model, whose caption calls it validated to about Mach 4.6 and asks you to treat anything above about Mach 10 as extrapolation. A curve reaching this range says nothing about heating, structural survival or ablation; the app computes none of them. See **Extrapolation**. [NASA: Mach number and flight regimes](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/airplane/mach.html).

### I–L

**Ignition event / ignition delay** — The trigger that lights a motor, and the extra time after that trigger before it lights. *In the app:* on a staged design (more than one stage, or a **Booster (parallel stage)**), each loaded motor's card on **Motors & Launch** has an **Ignition** selector with the delay in seconds beside it: **Automatic (launch / lower stage's ejection)** — the launch stage lights at launch, each stage above it on the ejection charge of the stage below — **Lower stage burnout + delay (electronics)**, **Launch + delay (timer)**, **Lower stage ejection charge + delay**, or **Never**. You set ignition separately from stage separation and recovery deployment. When you pick a motor for any stage above the launch stage, the app starts it on Automatic only if the catalogue records its propellant as black powder, which the charge below can light — composite and hybrid motors generally need an igniter. Every other motor there starts on lower-stage burnout + 1 s, including one with no recorded propellant, which covers every imported `.eng` or `.rse` motor. Launch-stage and strap-on motors start on Automatic. A design opened from a file keeps the ignition the file gives it (an `.ork` trigger the simulator does not know opens on Automatic, with a note). Treat the default as a starting point, and set the trigger your staging hardware will use. Picking a motor resets that mount's trigger, so choose your motor first. See *Multi-Stage and Clustered Rockets → Ignition triggers on a staged rocket*.

**Impulse class** — The letter band for a motor's total impulse. Each full letter's upper limit is twice the one before (A ends at 2.5 N·s, B at 5, C at 10), so one letter up does not mean twice the impulse: a C motor near the bottom of its band has barely more than a B near the top of its band. The class fixes total impulse alone; diameter, propellant, burn time and ignition method are separate facts you look up in the motor's own listing. *In the app:* the motor browser's **Class** chips, one per letter your mount can take, with the 1/4A and 1/2A motors under **A**, and each motor's diameter and burn time in the browser's **Diam** and **Burn (s)** columns; an imported `.eng` or `.rse` motor is lettered from its own curve's impulse. [NASA: motor designation](https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/model-rocket-engine-designation/).

**Impulse / total impulse, I** — Thrust added up over time: the area under the thrust curve, in newton-seconds (N·s). A constant 100 N for 2 s is 200 N·s. Impulse is not energy and is never quoted in joules, and motors with the same impulse can have very different thrust profiles. *In the app:* the motor browser's **Impulse (Ns)** column and the **Impulse (Ns)** window under **▸ All filters**. When the curve the app flies adds up to more than 5 % away from the motor's certified total impulse, a note gives both figures and says which way your apogee will lean. [ThrustCurve: motor statistics](https://www.thrustcurve.org/info/motorstats.html).

**Inner tube / motor mount** — An inner tube is a tube inside the airframe; a motor mount is a tube flagged to take a motor. *In the app:* you add an **Inner tube** to a body tube, and it arrives with **Acts as motor mount** ticked. For a minimum-diameter build, tick **Motor mount (motor loads in this tube)** on the body tube instead. An inner tube imported from a file is a mount only if the file marks it as one. Clear **Acts as motor mount** and the tube becomes plain structure: it keeps its mass and takes no motor. Only flagged tubes get a card in the **Motors** panel and a **motor** badge in the component tree. Set off the centerline with **Distance off centerline**, an inner tube adds roll inertia whether or not it holds a motor. See **Minimum-diameter / sub-minimum rocket**.

**Instance** — One physical copy made by a repeated assembly, such as each pod of a pod set. The copies share one design, so an edit changes them all, and their count multiplies mass, aerodynamic forces and motor count. *In the app:* **Instances (around body)** on a **Pod set** or a **Booster (parallel stage)** — the slider runs 1 to 8, and you can type up to 32. A motor card says where its extra motors come from ("×3 — one per pod"), and **Export .csv — component data** has a **Copies flown** column.

**Integration / integrator** — The numerical method that steps a flight forward through time from the forces on the rocket and its current motion. *In the app:* the kernel flies the rigid-body flight on RK4 with an adaptive step, capped by your **Time step (s)** (0.05 s when blank), and once a recovery device deploys it switches to a simpler descent stepper, with strides of up to half a second. The integrator moves the rocket; the aerodynamics model, a separate choice, supplies the forces. See **RK4 — fourth-order Runge–Kutta** and **Three degrees of freedom, 3DOF**.

**Interpolation** — Estimating a value between known data points. *In the app:* no readout carries the name, but the kernel reads a motor's thrust between the samples of its curve as a straight line and reads the air between the points of its 500 m atmosphere grid, and the app, working from the kernel's flight record, interpolates the reported rod-exit speed to the instant the rocket clears the guide. Interpolation only joins the points a file gives: a coarse or badly digitized curve flies exactly as coarse, so import a better `.eng` or `.rse` if yours looks wrong.

**ISA — International Standard Atmosphere** — A reference profile of temperature and pressure with altitude: 15 °C and 101,325 Pa at sea level, cooling 6.5 °C per km up to 11 km, with further layers above. *In the app:* your flight's air properties come from it. Leave **Temperature** or **Station pressure** blank under **Launch conditions** and the flight flies the ISA value for your **Site altitude**, shown grayed in the box; the **Density altitude** readout runs the profile backwards. It is a model atmosphere rather than any real day's weather: to fly a particular day, type measured pad values, or fetch the day's weather with **☁ Get weather…** and press **Apply**; that simulation then flies your values instead. See **Lapse rate**.

**Joint clearance / spigot** — A spigot is the short sleeve on one printed segment that is glued into the next segment's bore; the joint clearance is the gap on each side between them. *In the app:* **Joint clearance (per side)** under **Preferences → 3D printing**, once you pick a **Printer**: its starting 0.15 mm suits FDM parts and 30-minute epoxy, and 0.05 mm suits thin CA, which seizes in a wider gap. A part too tall for your printer then exports as numbered segments (**🖨 STL for printing — N pieces**) joined by spigots a quarter of the local diameter long, 12 to 30 mm. See *Files, Units, and Offline Use → Printing oversized parts on a 3D printer*.

**Kbf / body carryover** — The extra normal force the fins induce on the body tube they sit on: the body's side of the fin–body interference, the reverse of the body's effect on the fins' airflow. *In the app:* the term that **Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf)** adds — the default under Preferences → Aerodynamics → **Aerodynamics model**, shown as **Rogers Kbf** in the vitals strip's **Aero** selector. **Supersonic**, and so **Auto**, carries it in its own interference model; **Classic Extended Barrowman** leaves it out. On typical aft-finned designs it moves the CP aft and raises your margin — closer on the designs the model was validated against, but not the safer figure, since the app then shows more margin than Classic does. See **Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf)**.

**Kernel / simulation kernel** — The core of a simulator: the code that works out the forces, masses and motion and steps a flight forward in time. *In the app:* the code behind every **Launch**, every CG, CP and stability figure, and the **Drag analysis** sweep. It is OpenRocket's physics code, compiled to JavaScript so it runs in your browser, with the app's own changes on top. You meet the word in the **Drag analysis** caption, which calls sea-level air at 20 °C "the kernel default", and in the tip on **⬇ Flight data (.csv)**; this guide also calls it the simulator or the flight engine. See **OpenRocket / desktop OpenRocket**. See **Validation / verification**.

**Kinematic viscosity, ν** — Dynamic viscosity divided by air density, in m²/s: the viscosity term in `Re = V × L ÷ ν`. It is a property of the air, separate from any friction coefficient or surface finish. *In the app:* no field shows it. The kernel works it out each step from the local temperature and density, so it grows as the air thins, and at a given speed your Reynolds number falls with height. See **Reynolds number, Re**.

**Laminar / turbulent flow** — In a laminar boundary layer the air slides along in orderly layers; a turbulent one churns with irregular mixing and costs more skin friction. These are states of the flow, separate from surface-finish names. *In the app:* every simulation, in every aerodynamics model, treats the boundary layer as fully turbulent, whatever the finish (**Mirror surface (0 µm)** included) or the Reynolds number. Each part's **Surface finish** sets a roughness floor under its skin-friction coefficient, and the part pays whichever is higher. On a small, very smooth model, expect the skin-friction drag to lean high, by an unmeasured amount, since part of the real boundary layer may stay laminar. The gusting you set with **Wind avg** and **Wind gusts σ** is wind turbulence, a separate modeled effect. See **Boundary layer** and **Surface finish / roughness**.

**Landing rate** — How fast the rocket is coming straight down at ground contact. *In the app:* the **Landing rate** tile on Results, the launch report's **Landing descent rate** row, and its **Landing rate OK (≤ 20 ft/s)** check, red above 20 ft/s (6.1 m/s); each booster is judged against the same target. Speed over the ground, wind drift and all, is a separate figure: the optional **Ground speed at landing** tile. Passing the check is a screen: a fragile payload, a heavy airframe or hard ground may need your rocket to come down slower.

**Lapse rate** — How fast the air's temperature changes with height. In the lowest standard layer it falls 6.5 °C per km; above 11 km it holds steady, and higher still it rises, so one per-km figure fits one layer only. *In the app:* a blank **Temperature** flies the standard profile. Type your own pad temperature and the kernel runs a straight line from it to the standard −56.5 °C at 11 km, so a hot pad cools faster than 6.5 °C per km on the way up and a cold one slower.

**Lateral** — Sideways, in the horizontal plane: the horizontal part of a position, velocity or acceleration. **Lateral distance** is the straight-line horizontal distance from the launch point, shorter than the curved path flown. *In the app:* **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** has **Lateral distance**, **Lateral direction**, **Lateral velocity** and **Lateral acceleration** columns, and the launch report's **Landing distance from pad** is your lateral distance at touchdown. See **Drift / landing drift**.

**Latitude / longitude** — Your launch site's position on the Earth: latitude in degrees north (positive) or south (negative) of the equator, longitude east (positive) or west (negative). *In the app:* **Latitude (°)**, 28.61° to start, and **Longitude (°)**, at the foot of the **Launch conditions** panel. Latitude nudges the flight through gravity and the Coriolis term. Longitude moves no flight number: blank flies −80.6°, desktop OpenRocket's default, and the value fills the **Longitude** column of **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** and travels with the `.ork`. Type a map's 119.06° W as −119.06: a wrong sign puts your site in the other hemisphere, and **☁ Get weather…** then fetches that place's weather when you pick **This design’s site**, which the dialog offers once both fields hold your own values. Applying weather from any place writes that place's coordinates into both fields, since their rows arrive ticked; untick them to keep yours.

**Launch-guide exit velocity / rod-exit velocity** — The simulated speed at the moment the rocket clears the rod or rail: the handoff from mechanical guidance to aerodynamic control, since the fins need airflow to steer. *In the app:* the launch report's **Velocity at launch guide exit**, **Rod exit** in the batch and saved-run tables, and the optional **Guide departure** tile. The report's **Lift-off speed OK** check holds it against the app's 15 m/s (about 50 ft/s) guidance: below that, the speed row turns red and the report adds a rod-exit speed warning. **Thrust : weight at departure** is graded as a separate check. The app interpolates to the instant the rocket clears the guide, so its figure can read a few percent below desktop OpenRocket's for the same flight.

**Launch guide / rod / rail** — The rod, rail or tower that holds the rocket to one line while it gathers speed; a launch lug rides a rod, and rail buttons or a launch shoe ride a rail. *In the app:* **Rod length** (1 m by default), **Rod angle** and **Rod aim** under **Launch conditions**. The kernel holds the rocket to the guide's line until it has traveled the whole **Rod length**, then lets it fly free, rod or rail alike. Your lug or buttons add only drag and mass, so if the aft-most one sits well up the airframe, shorten **Rod length** by its distance from the rocket's aft end — otherwise rod-exit speed reads high, more than your rocket really gets. See **Rod aim**.

**Launch lug** — A short tube on the airframe that slides along a launch rod to guide the rocket. *In the app:* add a **Launch lug** to a body tube and set its **Length**, **Outer diameter** (**Outer radius** when Preferences → Display → **Round components entered as** is **Radius**), **Wall thickness**, position and **Angle around body**; it adds drag and mass. The guide's length is **Rod length** under **Launch conditions**, counted in full wherever the lug sits (see **Launch guide / rod / rail**); for rail hardware, see **Rail button**.

**Leading edge / trailing edge, LE / TE** — The front and rear edges of a fin. Their shape, sweep, thickness and bluntness all change drag; a chamfer is a beveled strip along an edge, and its length is a shape dimension of the same fin. *In the app:* the fin's **Cross section** (**Square**, **Rounded**, **Airfoil (pointed)**) sets how both edges are treated. Once you name a **Supersonic airfoil** section, the edge fields that section reads shape it too, under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**: **LE chamfer length** on **Hexagonal**, **Double wedge (diamond)** (where it places the thickest point) and **Hexagonal, blunt base**; **TE chamfer length** on **Hexagonal** only; and **LE bluntness radius** on every section except **NACA (round LE)**, which works out its own. See **LE bluntness radius**.

**LE bluntness radius** — The radius of a fin's rounded leading edge: 0 is a knife edge, and a larger radius a blunter one. *In the app:* the **LE bluntness radius** field on trapezoidal, elliptical and freeform fin sets. It counts once you name a **Supersonic airfoil** section other than **Classic (from cross section)** and fly **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** or **Supersonic**; it then adds swept-cylinder drag on a strip twice the radius tall, reduced by sweep: next to nothing at low subsonic speed, climbing steeply toward Mach 0.9 and staying high above it. A **NACA (round LE)** section uses its own radius, worked out from the fin's thickness and chord, in place of yours. It saves in `.ork` and `.CDX1` files. See **Leading edge / trailing edge, LE / TE**.

**Lift / normal force** — Lift acts at right angles to the oncoming air, normal force at right angles to the rocket's body axis; they are close at small angles of attack and part company as the angle grows. Stability work usually uses normal force and its moment about the CG. *In the app:* the stability readouts say **no lift yet**, with no CP, while your design makes no normal force — a tube with no nose cone and fewer than three fins, say. **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** carries the **Normal force coefficient**, and **⬇ Drag table (.csv)** lists CNα against Mach.

**Loaded mass / launch mass / pad mass** — The complete rocket's mass with its assigned motors installed, propellant and every modeled component included: its mass at the start of the flight, before any propellant burns or any stage separates. *In the app:* **Mass (loaded)** on the Design tab, **Mass** in the vitals strip, **Launch mass** in the launch report, and the optional **Pad weight** tile on Results. A weighed pad mass (**Weighed pad mass with this motor**, or **Weighed pad mass with every motor installed** on a rocket carrying more than one motor) reaches this total only through the hardware it implies: the app subtracts the dry rocket and the catalogue motors from your weighing and carries only what is left as hardware on the primary motor for the whole flight, so the loaded mass, the launch mass and the recovery weight all include that hardware. The dry rocket is your **Measured mass** if you entered one, otherwise the modeled dry mass. So the loaded and launch masses equal your scale reading only when that dry figure is also the one the rocket flies at: with no measured airframe mass entered, or once you have brought the model to it, for example with a **Build allowance**. See **Weighed pad mass** and **Primary mount**.

### M–N

**Mach-Alt table** — RASAero's list pairing each Mach number with an altitude, so a static drag sweep runs at the Reynolds numbers of a wind-tunnel test. *In the app:* the app reads one only from a RASAero .CDX1 you open, and the import note says so. The **Conditions** selector in the **Drag analysis** panel then offers **File Mach-Alt table**, which sweeps at the file's altitudes. It is not the altitude your flight reaches at each speed, and it changes drag analysis only — never a flight. The app holds it only while that design is open: a reload or **✕ New** drops it, and **Save .CDX1 — RASAero II** writes an empty table, so reopen the original file to get it back.

**Mach number, M** — Your rocket's airspeed divided by the local speed of sound: `M = V_air ÷ a`. Mach 1 is the speed of sound in the air around the rocket, not a fixed speed in mph or m/s, and temperature sets it: the same airspeed on a hot day and a cold day gives two different Mach numbers. *In the app:* the **Mach number** flight plot, **Max Mach** in the launch report, and the Mach axis of the **Drag analysis** panel. A **Temperature** you type under **Launch conditions** moves the Mach numbers your flight reaches; the **Drag analysis** sweep runs in the air its own **Conditions** selector names and does not follow it. [NASA: Mach number](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/airplane/mach.html).

**Main parachute** — The recovery device that sets your landing speed. In dual deployment it opens after the drogue, lower on the way down. *In the app:* the **Recovery sizing** panel takes the parachute whose **Deploy at** is **Altitude (descending)** as your main — with none set that way, the largest canopy, counting a chute with no **Canopy diameter** as the 0.3 m the app flies it at — and sizes it in its **Main** row for 15–20 ft/s, aiming at 18 ft/s. The flight works out the descent from every device already out, so a drogue still attached adds its drag to the main's. See **Drogue** and **Dual deployment**.

**Mass component / mass object** — A part that stands for a known mass of a given size at a given place: ballast, a battery, an altimeter, a payload. *In the app:* a **Mass component** with **Mass**, **Length**, **Diameter** (**Radius** if you set Preferences → Display → **Round components entered as** to Radius) and **Type**. **Type** (Altimeter, Battery, Payload and so on) is a label that travels through .ork files; choosing **Altimeter** or **Deployment charge** fires nothing — a parachute's **Deploy at** setting does that. **⌾ Estimate** measures straight past a mass component, so model an e-bay floor as a **Bulkhead**. The **Build allowance** is a mass component too.

**Mass override / CG override** — A mass or balance point you type in place of the one the app computes for a part. *In the app:* **Mass** and **CG from component top** under **Overrides (blank = calculated)** in the property panel; the CG counts from the front of that part, not from the nose tip. On a camera shroud the block is shown but ignored in flight; type its weight in **Mass (as built)**. On a part with others inside it, a **Use instead of everything inside** box appears once the field holds a value. Unticked, your figure stands in for that part's own figure only, and everything inside still counts. Ticked, it stands in for the part and everything in it, those parts' own overrides included. A stage, pod set or booster has no mass or CG of its own, so an unticked mass override there *adds* that mass on top of the contents, and an unticked CG only places that added mass — alone, it moves nothing, and the panel says **This is not doing anything yet**. On those, tick the box. An override leaves the geometry, parts and recovery devices underneath intact: clear it and the computed figure comes back. The motor sits outside every override — its mass and CG are always added on top — so leave the motor out of the figure you type. The Cd override has a box of its own that works the same way; see **Cd override**.

**Mass / weight** — Mass is how much matter there is; weight is the force gravity puts on it: `W = m × g`, in newtons in the physics equations. *In the app:* labels such as **Recovery weight** and the run table's **Pad Weight (g)** use the hobby's everyday wording but show a mass — in the unit you pick under Preferences (g, kg, oz or lb), except that those run-table columns always read in grams. Weight as a force appears in the launch report's **Thrust : weight OK** check, which sets the motor's thrust against it.

**Material density** — Mass per volume for a solid material, mass per area for a canopy sheet, or mass per length for a cord: three different quantities with different units, so a figure means something only beside its material type. *In the app:* a solid part's **Material** list fills in its **Material density** (kg/m³, shown in your density unit); a parachute or streamer has **Canopy material** (kg/m²), and a parachute's **Line material** or a shock cord's **Cord material** is in kg/m. Typing your own density sets the list to **Custom**. The app applies the density to the volume, area or length it works out from the part's dimensions, so your mass is only as good as those dimensions.

**Max motor length** — The longest motor a stage's airframe has room for. It is separate from the diameter fit: a motor can be the right diameter and still too long. *In the app:* the design value is **Max motor length (blank = no limit)** on an inner-tube motor mount, and it travels with the .ork. The **Max motor length** field on **Motors & Launch** overrides it per stage, and it is the only place to set one for a body-tube (minimum-diameter) mount. The motor browser flags longer motors with ⚠ and keeps them selectable; **only motors that fit** hides them. Batch simulation leaves them out. **⌾ Estimate** measures forward from the aft end of the mount, adjusted by the **Motor overhang** (a recessed motor loses room), to the first engine block or bulkhead, the aft end of the nose cone or of a transition that narrows going forward — or else the front of the stage, or of the pod or strap-on the mount sits in. It searches only the mount's own airframe, so a pod's nose cone is out of the core motor's way; with several mounts in one stage it takes the shortest result, and the **Room for** line names what it stopped at. It measures straight past mass components, centering rings, couplers and recovery gear, so an avionics sled modeled as a mass component leaves the room open: model the e-bay's aft bulkhead if you want a real number. The estimate also cannot see wadding, a baffle modeled as some other part, or how tightly the recovery gear is packed.

**Measured mass & CG** — Your finished airframe's weighed mass and balance point, with the motor out. *In the app:* the **Measured mass & CG** box under the component tree on the Design workspace. Type **Measured mass** and **Measured CG from nose tip**, or open a single-stage .rkt that records them and they fill in. The box shows your gap to the **Computed mass** and **Computed CG**. When mass added at one point along the rocket would close it, the box says how much and where; **Add “Build allowance”** inserts that mass as a component, and **Update “Build allowance”** changes the existing one rather than adding a second. Nothing changes until you press the button. When no added mass can close the gap, the box says so — a sign a part mass is wrong. When a mass override with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked would cancel mass added there, the box offers no Build allowance: it names that part, and when that part is a stage and the only stage whose mass override covers everything inside it, the box offers **Pin “…” to my measured mass & CG**, which sets that stage's mass and CG overrides to your measured figures. The two figures save with the .ork and a share link, and **✕ New** and **⤢ Scale…** clear them. Use **Weighed pad mass** for a weighing made with the flight's motors installed.

**Minimum-diameter / sub-minimum rocket** — A minimum-diameter rocket has no separate motor mount tube: the motor loads straight into the airframe tube. In a sub-minimum build the motor case itself is the outer airframe: fins bond straight to the case, or propellant is cast into the airframe tube. *In the app:* for minimum diameter, tick **Motor mount (motor loads in this tube)** on the body tube, and motors are fitted against its bore. For sub-minimum, model the body tube at the case's own outside diameter and length, tick that box, then tick **Sub-minimum: motor case is the airframe**, which appears once the first box is ticked. Motors are then fitted against the tube's outside diameter, in the motor browser and in batch simulation alike. The case's weight belongs in the motor's mass, not in the tube: set the tube's **Wall thickness** to 0 unless the airframe adds real structure over the case, and check that the motor file's weight includes the case. If it leaves out hardware, enter a **Weighed pad mass** rather than thickening the tube. The setting changes only which diameter the app fits motors against; the simulation flies whatever motor you load.

**M+ mark** — A small mark that tells you **Auto** has moved your design onto the supersonic model. *In the app:* **M+** sits in the vitals strip just after the **Aero** selector while it reads **Auto**, once an Auto flight has passed Mach 0.9 or its short trial run said it would. From then on your design's stability, drag analysis and flights use **Supersonic**, and the launch report's **Aero model** row reads **Supersonic (auto — flight exceeded Mach 0.9)**. The mark clears when you change the design, its motors or the launch conditions, and your next Auto flight decides again. See **Auto (aerodynamics)**.

**Moment of inertia** — Resistance to turning faster or slower about a given axis. It depends on the mass and on how far that mass lies from the axis, so matching mass and CG alone leaves inertia unmatched. *In the app:* **Roll inertia (loaded)**, about the long axis, and **Pitch inertia (loaded)**, about the transverse axis used for pitch and yaw, in kg·m² in the **▤ All stats** drawer; the **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** download names them **Rotational moment of inertia** and **Longitudinal moment of inertia**. A mass override with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked scales that part's inertia to the mass you gave it. On a spaced cluster or any inner tube off the centerline, with or without a motor in it, **Roll inertia (loaded)** reads higher than desktop OpenRocket 24.12 gives, in every aerodynamics model: the app counts the tubes' spread that 24.12 leaves out.

**Moment / torque** — A force's turning effect about a point or axis: the force times its distance from it. The same normal force turns the rocket harder the farther from the CG it acts. Aerodynamic moment coefficients describe that effect with the dynamic pressure, reference area and reference length divided out. *In the app:* moments appear only as coefficients, in the **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** and **⬇ Flight data + charts (.xlsx)** downloads: **Pitch moment coefficient**, **Yaw moment coefficient** and **Roll moment coefficient**, with **Pitch damping coefficient**, **Roll damping coefficient** and **Roll forcing coefficient** beside them (yaw has no damping column).

**Motor case** — The reusable casing a reload motor is assembled into, named by its maker's case designation. *In the app:* the **Motor case** row under the launch report's **Show all details**, and the **Case** column of the **⬇ Run table (.csv)** and **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)** downloads. It shows the case thrustcurve.org lists for the motor — `RMS-29/180` for an AeroTech H128W, `Pro38-6GXL` for a Cesaroni 1008J420 — so you can check you own that hardware. Most single-use motors, many hybrids, a few reloads and motors you import yourself list no case: the report row shows a dash and the run table's **Case** cell is left empty. A single-use motor that does list one shows a size or line name, such as `SU 29x85` or `DMS`. See **Case / casing / closure** and **Reload / single-use motor**.

**Motor designation / common name** — The motor's identifying code. In a simple code such as `C6-5`, C is the impulse class, 6 is the nominal average thrust in newtons, and 5 is the nominal delay in seconds from burnout to the ejection charge (see **Ejection delay**). *In the app:* the motor catalogue keeps two different names. The *common name* is just the class and thrust (`D10`). The manufacturer's *designation* can carry more: propellant letters (AeroTech `D10W`), a delay (`F115SN-12A`), or Cesaroni's total-impulse prefix (`381I224-15A`). The motor browser's **Motor** column shows the designation lightly tidied: Cesaroni's total-impulse prefix and the `HP-` prefix AeroTech and Loki use are dropped, so that Cesaroni reads `I224-15A`. Once loaded, a catalogue motor is labeled by its common name plus the chosen delay — `C6-5`, `C6-P` for a plugged motor, or `C6 (auto delay)` — in the vitals strip's **Motor** item. The launch report's title and the run table's **Designation** column show the tidied designation instead (`D10W`, `I224-15A`), and the run table gives the delay you flew in its **Delay (s)** column. An imported motor uses its file's designation for both names. A motor's tested average thrust can differ from the rounded figure in its name. [NASA: model rocket engine designation](https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/model-rocket-engine-designation/).

**Motor overhang** — How far the motor's aft end sits past the aft end of its mount. *In the app:* the **Motor overhang (past aft end)** field on a motor mount — an inner tube, or a body tube ticked as the motor mount. A positive value means the motor sticks out. A negative value means the motor sits recessed inside the mount, for example so a retainer cap can close over it. It moves where the motor's mass sits in the simulation and where the motor is drawn in the 2D and 3D views. It also lengthens or shortens the room the **⌾ Estimate** button beside **Max motor length** on Motors & Launch measures: a recessed motor loses that much room. It is separate from the mount's length and from any nozzle diameter.

**Newton, N / newton-second, N·s** — A newton is a unit of force: 1 N accelerates 1 kg at 1 m/s². A newton-second is force kept up over time, the unit of impulse; `N/s` would mean force changing per second, the wrong unit for total impulse. *In the app:* the **Thrust** and **Drag force** flight plots stay in newtons whatever units you pick, and the motor browser's **Impulse (Ns)** column gives total impulse in newton-seconds. See **Impulse / total impulse, I**.

**Normal-force coefficient, CN / Cn** — The aerodynamic force across the rocket's axis, divided by dynamic pressure and reference area. CNα is its slope with angle of attack, usually per radian: at small angles `CN ≈ CNα × α`, with α in radians. CN and CNα are not interchangeable — one is a force coefficient at one condition, the other describes how that coefficient changes with angle. *In the app:* **Normal force coefficient** is a column of the **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** download, and CNα against Mach, per radian, is the `cna_per_rad` column of the **Drag analysis** panel's **⬇ Drag table (.csv)** — the one to give a trajectory program that asks you for CNα.

**Nose cone / nose profile** — The forward body that closes your airframe, and the curve its outline follows. *In the app:* a **Nose cone**'s **Shape**, shared with transitions, is **Ogive**, **Conical**, **Ellipsoid**, **Parabolic**, **Haack** or **Power**. Ogive, Parabolic, Haack and Power add a **Shape parameter** that picks one curve from that family for the same length and base diameter; Conical and Ellipsoid have one fixed profile. The shoulder (**Shoulder diameter**, **Shoulder length** and **Shoulder thickness**; the first reads **Shoulder radius** if you set Preferences → Display → **Round components entered as** to Radius) is the plug that fits into the tube below. It adds to the nose's mass only when it has a **Shoulder thickness**, typed or carried by a file you opened; left blank it flies 0 and weighs nothing. See **Shoulder / coupler**. It sits inside the airframe, outside the exposed aerodynamic profile. See **Shape parameter** and **Haack / Von Kármán**.

**Nozzle** — The shaped gas passage at a motor's aft end that speeds up and directs its exhaust. Its narrowest section is the throat; the opening where the gas leaves is the exit. The two sizes serve different purposes. *In the app:* the exit is the only nozzle size the app uses, as one **Nozzle exit diameter** per stage. See **Nozzle exit diameter** and **Nozzle throat / throat diameter**. [NASA: rocket thrust equations and nozzle geometry](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/BGP/rktthsum.html).

**Nozzle exit diameter** — The diameter across the gas opening at the nozzle's exit plane — not the throat, the motor case or the outside of the nozzle part. *In the app:* **Nozzle exit diameter** under each stage on **Motors & Launch**, the same field as **Nozzle exit diameter (drives thrust and drag; 0 = off)** on the stage in the property panel. It fills itself in from AeroTech's or Loki's published figure, or from an imported .rse that states one, and it follows the motor when you change it. Under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**, its area lowers base drag while the stage's motor burns — off the stage's aft-most base only, so a pod's base or a step keeps its full drag — and adds pressure thrust as the air thins; **Classic Extended Barrowman** ignores it. For a cluster, see **Equivalent nozzle exit diameter**. Two limits to expect: a staggered cluster gets its whole summed area from the moment its first motor lights, and a strap-on booster has no nozzle field of its own, so its motors fly with neither the base-drag reduction nor the pressure thrust, and the core stage's filled-in exit leaves them out.

**Nozzle throat / throat diameter** — The nozzle's narrowest internal gas passage. Its area matters to how the motor runs inside. *In the app:* the flight corrections use the exit area alone, and a throat diameter in an imported .rse is left unread. Loki's nozzles are banded: the number engraved on one is its throat in 64ths of an inch, and one exit serves a whole run of throat sizes. So a nozzle number tied to throat size is no substitute for a measured or published exit diameter; type the exit. See **Nozzle exit diameter**.

### O–P

**Ogive: tangent / secant** — A nose-cone or transition profile drawn as a circular arc. A tangent ogive meets the body tube smoothly; a secant ogive meets it at an angle. *In the app:* choose **Ogive** in a nose cone's or transition's **Shape** list, and a **Shape parameter** field (0–1) appears beside it. At 1, the default a blank field flies, you get a tangent ogive; below 1, the shallower kind of secant ogive, which always stays inside the tube's diameter; at 0, a straight cone. On a transition the smooth join is at the wider end only. The parameter changes the geometry your flight flies, and the 2D and 3D views draw that same profile. See **Shape parameter**.

**OOP (out of production)** — A motor its maker no longer produces, as thrustcurve.org lists it. *In the app:* the motor browser leaves out-of-production motors off its list until you tick **include out-of-production** under **▸ All filters**, and **Batch simulate** has its own **include OOP** box; both start unticked. In the browser's table an **OOP** badge follows the motor's name. Motors thrustcurve.org lists as occasionally produced are not out of production: they stay on the list with the box unticked, and carry no badge. After **↻ Check thrustcurve.org**, a motor the live catalogue has dropped is kept and marked out of production, so a design that names it still finds it.

**Opening shock** — The jolt when a recovery device opens and slows the rocket. The speed at that moment is one part of it; how fast the canopy inflates, the rocket's mass, line stretch, and how the bay and harness are built all matter too. *In the app:* the launch report grades opening speed. Its **Safe deployment** row reads **✓ yes** up to 70 ft/s and **⚠ NO** above that, and the recovery table's **Verdict** column reads **⚠ hard opening** for that device in either band. The report's comment line tells the bands apart: a caution up to 90 ft/s and a warning past it. The simulator adds a stricter flag of its own, shown as a △ note, whenever a device opens faster than 20 m/s (65.6 ft/s). The app works out no load on your shock cord or airframe, so treat a pass as a speed check and size that hardware yourself. See **Deployment velocity / opening speed**.

**Open-Meteo** — A free weather service, [open-meteo.com](https://open-meteo.com/), that publishes forecasts, a historical archive reaching back to 1940, and ground heights. *In the app:* **☁ Get weather…** in **Launch conditions** asks it for one hour's temperature, station pressure and 10 m wind at your place, and shows them beside your current values; nothing changes until you press **Apply**. Only what the lookup needs goes out: a place name you search for, with the country set in the dialog's **Country** list (none when it reads **Any country**); then the chosen place's coordinates to three decimals, the dates around your day and the altitude to fetch the air for. Nothing about your rocket is sent, and each request reaches Open-Meteo from your IP address. See **Get weather (☁ Get weather…)** and *Launch Conditions → Getting the day's weather*.

**OpenRocket / desktop OpenRocket** — The free, open-source model-rocket design and flight-simulation program written in Java by the OpenRocket project ([openrocket.info](https://openrocket.info)). *In the app:* MMRocket Sim is a separate program built on OpenRocket release 24.12: that release's physics kernel, compiled to JavaScript, computes every flight you run, with the app's own additions on top, among them extra aerodynamic terms and pressure thrust. The .ork file format is OpenRocket's, and the app opens and saves it; desktop OpenRocket ignores the app's own additions — among them the camera shroud, the protuberance, a parachute's spill hole, the nozzle exit diameter, the **Supersonic airfoil** section and its edge fields, the weighed pad mass, the **Measured mass & CG** figures and **Max motor length**. Desktop OpenRocket flies a vented canopy at its full typed Cd, so it descends slower there than here. For the numbers closest to desktop OpenRocket's, fly **Classic Extended Barrowman**; a few differences remain even there, such as the app's fixed turbulence seed. See **Classic Extended Barrowman**.

**Opt. delay** — Short for optimum ejection delay: the coast from motor burnout to apogee, so a charge fired after that long opens the recovery at the top of the flight. The simulator measures it on a probe flight with no deployment, to the ballistic apogee. *In the app:* the **Opt. delay** column in **Saved simulations** and **Batch simulate** shows it to 0.1 s, and the run details add "recommended (drill to fit)": the optimum rounded to the nearest whole second, never snapped to the delays the motor is sold with, since you drill a delay to any whole second. To fly the delay you drilled, pick **Custom (drilled)…** in the motor browser's **Delay** list, or type it into the mount's **Ejection delay (s)** field. **Auto (optimal)** in that list, or **auto (optimal)** on the top motor's card, flies the recommendation on the primary mount only. Any other mount left on auto shows **auto — top motor only** and flies the number in its **Ejection delay (s)** field: the motor's longest listed delay, or 0 s when the motor lists none, which fires the charge at burnout. Type that mount's delay yourself. See **Delay** and **Primary mount**.

**Override** — A value you supply in place of one the app would otherwise work out or assume, such as a component's mass, CG or drag coefficient, or the launch site's temperature. You can type one yourself, and some catalogue parts and imported files set one for you. *In the app:* a component's overrides sit under **Overrides (blank = calculated)** in its property panel — **Mass**, **CG from component top** and **Drag coefficient (Cd)** — and clearing one brings the computed value straight back. A protuberance has no such block: you set its mass and Cd in its own fields (see **Protuberance**). A camera shroud shows the block, but nothing typed there reaches the flight: type its weight in **Mass (as built)**. See **Camera shroud / fairing**. Once one holds a value on a part with others inside it, a **Use instead of everything inside** checkbox sets how far it reaches. Ticked, your figure replaces the component and everything in it. Unticked, it replaces only that component's own figure. A stage, pod set or booster has no figure of its own, so an unticked mass or Cd on one of these adds to what its contents compute; an unticked CG only positions that added mass, so with no mass override it moves nothing, and the panel says so. The vitals strip's **Aero** selector uses the word differently: its session override replaces your Preferences setting for the current session only. See **Mass override / CG override**.

**Packed diameter / packed length** — The size of a parachute or streamer folded and stowed, as its maker publishes it: the bundle, as opposed to the open canopy. *In the app:* each canopy the **Recovery sizing** panel lists shows "packs D × L". The panel compares each packed diameter with the bore (inside diameter) of the tube the chute sits in — or, for a chute outside a tube, the widest body tube's — leaves out the canopies that are too wide, and says how many it left out. It shows the packed length without checking it against your bay's length, so check that yourself. A canopy with no published packed size stays on the list, marked "packed size unpublished ‡", for you to check. With no body tube to measure against, the panel keeps every canopy and leaves every fit to you.

**Parallel booster / strap-on booster** — A separable stage mounted beside the central airframe. After it separates, it flies and lands on its own simulated branch, with whatever recovery gear you put inside it. It differs from a serial lower stage, which sits under the rocket, and from a **Pod set**, which stays attached. *In the app:* add a **Booster (parallel stage)** to a body tube. **Instances (around body)** sets how many ring the airframe (the slider runs to 8; you can type up to 32), **Radial distance**, **Radial reference** and **Angle around body** place them, and **Separate at** and **Separation delay** time the separation. The nozzle exit field belongs to the rocket's main stages, so a strap-on's motors get no power-on drag reduction and no pressure thrust.

**Parent / child / subtree** — How parts relate in the component tree. The parent is the part that holds another; its children sit directly inside it or on it; a subtree is a part with everything below it. *In the app:* the tree in the **Design** workspace's left column shows these relationships, and a part's **Position (in parent)** is measured along its parent. **Duplicate**, **Copy**, **Cut** and **Delete** each take the whole subtree, and an override with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked stands in for it. The tree offers only the children a parent can hold: fins and inner tubes go in a body tube, an engine block in an inner tube.

**Payload** — Something the rocket carries for its own sake, such as an instrument package or an experiment. *In the app:* model it with ordinary parts or a **Mass component**, whose **Type** list includes **Payload**. That label is for your records and travels through .ork files; the physics uses only the part's mass, size and position, so calling an item a payload leaves the aerodynamics and the recovery rules exactly as they were.

**Pink noise** — Random variation whose slow swings are stronger than its fast ones, unlike white noise, which is equally strong at every rate. *In the app:* only the guide uses the term — no panel or readout names it. It is how the simulator builds gusts around **Wind avg**, with **Wind gusts σ** setting their size. A two-pole pink-noise filter, sampled every 0.05 s and seeded from a fixed number, makes them, so a re-run repeats exactly. Measured in the app, the simulated wind forgets itself in about 0.65 s, where real gusts near the ground last many seconds: σ puts a reasonable amount of turbulence into your flight, shaken much faster than real air shakes it. See **Turbulence intensity** and *How It Works: Physics & Math → Wind, turbulence, and why a re-run repeats exactly*.

**Pitch inertia (loaded)** — The rocket's resistance to being swung nose-up, nose-down or sideways, with its motor loaded: its moment of inertia about the CG, across the long axis. *In the app:* a readout in **All stats** on the Design workspace, in kg·m², to four significant figures; the same figure serves pitch and yaw. It governs how quickly the rocket turns into the wind, so an error in it shows in your drift more than in your apogee. A mass override with **Use instead of everything inside** ticked scales the covered parts' inertia along with their mass. See **Moment of inertia** and **Roll inertia (loaded)**.

**Pitch / yaw / roll** — The three ways a rocket rotates. Pitch and yaw tip its long axis in two directions at right angles to each other, changing where the nose points; roll spins it about that axis, changing which fin faces which way. *In the app:* the launch report shows **Max roll rate** (in r/s and °/s) once the rocket rolls measurably, as it does with canted fins; **Roll inertia (loaded)** and **Pitch inertia (loaded)** sit in **All stats**; and **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** carries **Pitch rate**, **Yaw rate** and **Roll rate** at every step. The app uses one inertia figure for both pitch and yaw. See **Roll rate**.

**Planform / planform area** — A shape's outline, and the area inside it, projected flat: square-on to a fin's flat face, or side-on to a body (for a plain body tube, length × diameter). A fin's planform is a different thing from its thickness profile, its frontal area and its total wetted area. *In the app:* the freeform fin editor is titled **Fin planform**, and the 1:1 fin template prints that outline. The simulator uses the fin planform area for fin lift and the body's planform area for the body-lift (Galejs) term; pitch damping and the tumble-descent model use both, so each area feeds its own aerodynamic contributions. See **Body lift / Galejs correction**.

**Plate angle** — How far an inclined flat plate, such as a fin bracket or an anchor, tilts away from the body axis. *In the app:* on a **Protuberance (drag bump)** in the **Inclined flat plate** drag class, **Plate angle from the body** (0–90°, starting at 45°) sets it; the field appears only for that class. At 0° the plate lies flat along the body and adds no drag; at 90° it stands square to the airflow. The angle changes only the drag coefficient, 1.17·sin²θ, while the frontal area stays width × height × count at any angle. A positive value in **Cd on frontal area (blank or 0 = from class)** replaces the class figure, and the angle then does nothing. It is a separate setting from **Angle around body**, which sets where the part sits around the tube, and from a fin's **Cant angle**.

**Plugged motor / P delay** — A motor flown with no ejection charge: a factory `-P` motor, or any motor whose charge you removed so electronics can deploy the recovery. A `P` in a motor's delay list means plugged, and so does any listed delay of 90 s or more, the way RASP and RockSim files write "no charge". *In the app:* plugged is a setting of its own rather than a delay length. Pick **Plugged — no ejection charge** in the motor browser's **Delay** list (every motor offers it), or tick **plugged** beside a mount's **Ejection delay (s)**; tables show the delay as P. A plugged motor fires no ejection event, so it never deploys a recovery device set to **Motor ejection charge** and never separates a stage set to **This stage's ejection charge**. Unless another motor in that stage still has its charge, set those to **Apogee** or **Altitude (descending)**. The launch report reminds you for the primary mount's motor only (see **Primary mount**). In **Batch simulate** with **optimal delay per motor** unticked, a motor sold only plugged flies plugged, except where your chute waits for the charge: that row flies the optimum delay instead, marked **· opt.**

**Pod set** — One or more off-axis assemblies that stay attached to the rocket for the whole flight, such as side pods or an external raceway tube. Pods contribute their modeled mass and aerodynamic effects throughout the flight. *In the app:* add a **Pod set** to a body tube and build one pod inside it — its nose cone, tubes and fins — and the app repeats that pod at every instance. **Instances (around body)** sets how many (the slider runs to 8; you can type up to 32), and **Radial distance**, **Radial reference** and **Angle around body** place them. A motor in a pod fires in every pod, and a chute in a pod opens in each one. Use a **Parallel booster / strap-on booster** when the assembly should separate as a stage.

**Position relative to / Offset** — Where a part sits along its parent, measured from the parent's top, middle or bottom. *In the app:* under **Position (in parent)** in the property panel, **Relative to** offers **Top of parent**, **Middle of parent** or **Bottom of parent**, and **Offset** moves the part from there, positive toward the tail and negative toward the nose. Top lines up the two front edges, Middle the two centers, Bottom the two aft edges. Dragging the offset slider snaps to the parent's ends and middle, to other parts' edges and to a fin tab's edges. Nose cones, body tubes and transitions stack in order down their stage, so they carry no position fields. See **Parent / child / subtree**.

**Power-on / power-off drag** — Drag while a motor is thrusting, and drag while it is not. While the motor burns, its exhaust fills part of the base behind it, so boost drag is lower than coast drag. *In the app:* once a stage has a **Nozzle exit diameter**, the simulator takes that exit area off the drag of the stage's aft-most base while its motor burns, under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**; under **Classic Extended Barrowman** the field changes nothing. A pod's base, or a step where a wider part meets a narrower one, keeps its full base drag. The **Drag analysis** panel draws **CD power-on** beside **CD power-off**. This is separate from **Pressure thrust**, which changes thrust rather than drag.

**Pressure altitude / barometric altitude** — Pressure altitude is the height in a standard atmosphere that matches a measured pressure. A barometric rocket altimeter estimates height from how much the pressure falls, usually relative to its reading on the pad, and assumes standard air to do it. When the day's air differs from that profile, the estimate differs from the true height: on a day warmer than standard it reads low, on a colder day high. *In the app:* the altitudes the app reports are heights the simulated rocket reaches in the air you set, with no pressure-altitude conversion. So before you judge the simulation against an altimeter's apogee from a hot day, expect the altimeter to read below the true height. See **Station pressure** and **Density altitude**. [NWS: pressure altitude](https://forecast.weather.gov/glossary.php?letter=p).

**Pressure drag** — Drag caused by pressure differences over a shape, including the effects of bluntness, flow separation and compressibility (wave drag). *In the app:* the **Drag analysis** panel's **Breakdown (power-off)** chart, viewed **By type**, labels it **Pressure / wave**, beside **Friction** and **Base**; **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** names it **Pressure drag coefficient**. Both show base drag separately, even though base drag is also caused by a pressure difference, so add the two when you compare with a source that lumps them together.

**Pressure thrust** — The part of a motor's thrust that comes from the pressure difference between the nozzle exit and the surrounding air. *In the app:* the simulator adds it only when a stage has a **Nozzle exit diameter** and the aerodynamics model is **Rogers Kbf** (Rogers Modified Barrowman, the default), **Auto** or **Supersonic**; it is off under **Classic Extended Barrowman**. The app assumes each published curve was measured at 101,325 Pa and adds `A_exit × (101325 − P_ambient)`, once per stage, while the curve's own thrust is above zero. Lower air pressure gives a positive correction; air above 101,325 Pa gives a small negative one, and that correction can never pull the total thrust below zero. The **Thrust** plot and the report's **Thrust : weight at departure** include it, and the launch report says when it applied. The sea-level reference is an assumption the app makes, not a measured test condition for every curve. [NASA: rocket thrust equation](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/BGP/rktthsum.html).

**Primary mount** — The one motor mount the app treats as carrying the rocket's main motor. *In the app:* it is the topmost stage's mount that has a motor — within a stage, the core's mount before a pod set's, and a pod set's before a strap-on's; between two of the same kind, the one that got its motor first (in a file you open, the file's order). Its motor is the one the vitals strip's **Motor** readout names, the only one **Auto (optimal)** re-flies, and the one the launch report checks for a plugged charge. The weighed pad mass field appears on its card alone, so the **Hardware mass** rides on its motor. See **Weighed pad mass**.

**Propellant / propellant mass** — The material a motor burns to make its exhaust, and how much of it the motor starts with. Propellant mass is part of the loaded motor mass and is gone by burnout; it is a different figure from the motor's total loaded mass and from the hardware left at burnout. *In the app:* the simulator takes the propellant off as the motor burns, in proportion to the impulse delivered so far, or by an imported `.rse`'s own per-point masses where it turns RockSim's mass model off. Under **▸ All filters**, the motor browser's **Propellant** row offers a chip, with its count, for each of the propellants most used by motors that fit your mount; a chip you set hides any motor with no propellant named, such as an imported EX motor. The launch report's details name the flown motor's **Propellant**.

**Protuberance** — A bump or appendage sticking out into the airflow, such as a cable tunnel, a launch shoe or a camera housing. *In the app:* add a **Protuberance (drag bump)** to a body tube and give it a **Drag class**, **Width (across body)**, **Height (off the surface)** and **How many (identical)**. A positive Cd you type in **Cd on frontal area (blank or 0 = from class)** replaces the class's figure. Aerodynamically it adds drag on that frontal area only: no lift and no shift in CP, unlike a **Camera shroud / fairing**, which flies as a lifting strake. Its **Mass, all of them (0 = not counted)** is flown at the part's center, and **Length along body (shape only, no drag)** sets the drawn shape and where that center falls, with no effect on drag. Pick the component type that matches the effect you are modeling. See **Plate angle** and *Designing the Rocket → Protuberances — the bumps that only make drag*.

### Q–R

**Quaternion** — A four-number way of storing which way a body points in three dimensions. The flight engine keeps your rocket's orientation as one, so the rocket can pitch over at apogee without the gimbal-lock singularities of angle-based schemes; if your rocket goes unstable with no chute out, the engine hands it to a separate, simpler tumble model. *In the app:* it stays inside the engine, and nothing you type sets it; the **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** download gives orientation as the angles **Θ — Vertical orientation — zenith** and **Φ — Lateral orientation — azimuth** (see **Azimuth / zenith angle**).

**Quick picks** — A short list of common Estes motors for one-click loading while you try the app out. *In the app:* the **Quick picks** dropdown on a motor mount's card on **Motors & Launch** offers the Estes A8-3, B6-4 and C6-5 — ordinary catalogue motors with their certified curves. The list is filtered to your mount, and the starter rocket's mount is 18 mm, so its fourth pick, the 24 mm D12-5, never shows. The dropdown appears only while your design is the untouched starter rocket, **My Rocket**; rename or change the design and the field becomes a plain **Motor** readout, and your motors come from **🔎 Browse motors / import EX (.eng, .rse)…**.

**Radial reference** — How the distance of a pod set or strap-on booster from the airframe is measured. *In the app:* the **Radial reference** menu on a **Pod set** or **Booster (parallel stage)**, beside **Radial distance**. **Gap from parent surface**, the default, measures from the surface of the body tube the assembly rides on out to the assembly's widest nose cone, body tube or transition, so 0 means touching. **From parent centerline** measures axis to axis. Switching keeps the number in **Radial distance** and reads it the other way, so your pod moves: re-enter the distance after you switch.

**Rail button** — A small standoff fitting that slides in a launch rail's slot and guides the rocket until it leaves the rail. *In the app:* one **Rail button** component stands for a set, with a **Number of rail buttons**, a **Distance between buttons** and one **Angle around body**, and every button in it is drawn, weighed and flown. **📍 Auto-place rail buttons** puts the forward button at the CG and the aft one about an inch from the aft end; press it again after the CG moves. Type your buttons' real sizes in **Outer diameter**, **Total height**, **Inner (waist) diameter** (the narrow neck that rides in the rail's slot), **Base / standoff height**, **Flange height** and **Screw-head height**. A blank field flies a generic 9.7 mm button's figure, every field but **Screw-head height** (mass only) feeds the button's drag, and that drag grows faster than its height. The Design tab warns when a fin, lug, camera shroud, protuberance, pod set or strap-on booster sits on a button's line, or when buttons sit at different angles, because the rail needs one clear, straight path.

**RASAero II** — Rogers Aeroscience's aerodynamic-analysis and flight-simulation program, whose design files end in `.CDX1`; its "Rogers Modified Barrowman" method inspired the app's default model. *In the app:* **Open…** reads a `.CDX1`, and **Save As / Export → Save .CDX1 — RASAero II** writes one. The file brings geometry, finish, motors, nozzle exits and the author's stated launch weight and CG, but no materials or per-part masses — walls come in at a stand-in 2 mm, so review your masses before you trust the numbers. The app flies its own aerodynamics, reading past the file's CD override and model settings. Export takes at most 3 stages; one fin set per tube, of 3–8 trapezoid fins (a freeform set goes out only when its outline is a plain three- or four-point trapezoid, and elliptical or tube fins are refused); conical transitions and boat tails; and any nose shape except **Parabolic**. If your design falls outside those limits, the save stops and a note tells you why.

**Recovery mass / recovery weight** — The mass a recovery device has to lower: the rocket, or the part of it that comes down on its own, with its retained motors, including unburned propellant, and without any stage that has already separated — different from the dry mass without a motor, and usually below the pad mass. *In the app:* the **Recovery weight** tile in the Design workspace's All stats grid, also on the phone Fly screen, is the sustainer's, with any stage set to separate at **Never** still attached. It reads **load a motor** until you load one, includes the adapter and closure your weighed pad mass adds (see **Weighed pad mass**), and counts a motor whose **Ignition** is **Never** at its full loaded weight, because it comes down unburned. The **Recovery sizing** panel adds a section for each stage that comes down on its own. A **Booster (parallel stage)** that separates blanks the tile and the panel's recommendation, for your sustainer's canopy as well, because the app cannot say which mass stays with the sustainer; one set to **Never** stays bolted on and is weighed with the rocket, like a pod set. If a separating booster stage carries a pod set or a strap-on ring set to **Never**, with two or more copies in either case, the app cannot weigh that booster, so its figure is blank — and the sustainer's is blank too when the sustainer also carries one. The launch report's **Recovery weight (after burnout)**, which is also the optional **Recovery weight** tile you can add to the Results workspace with **⚙ Choose metrics** and a column of the **⬇ Run table** downloads, is a separate, simulated figure: the mass once the last burnout, the last separation and the first deployment are past, shown for every design, strap-ons included. So the **Recovery weight** tile in Results can read differently from the one in Design, and the **Recovery sizing** panel works from the Design figure.

**Recovery sizing** — Working out what canopy size gives a safe descent, from the recovery weight, a target descent rate, the air density and the canopy's Cd. A diameter means something only beside the Cd it was worked out with. *In the app:* the **Recovery sizing** panel, under the component properties on the right of the Design workspace, leads each of its **Main** and **Drogue** sections with a size, such as "about 66 in at Cd 2.13", aimed at 18 ft/s for a main and 60 ft/s for a drogue. The Cd is the one the chute in that role flies — the Cd it states, or the automatic 0.8 when it states none — corrected for that chute's spill hole. With no parachute in the role, the panel uses your design's other chute's Cd, or failing that the 0.8, and it says which it used. A chute set to deploy at an altitude is the main; otherwise the bigger canopy is. Streamers are left out, because a streamer's Cd is referenced to its strip area, not a diameter. It then lists catalogue canopies whose packed size fits the bay, with the descent rate each gives your rocket in your launch site's air. Treat it as an estimate: the canopy maker's own sizing and deployment limits still apply.
