**Reference area, A_ref / Ar** — The area that turns an aerodynamic force into a coefficient. The rocket's reference is a circle, `A_ref = π × D_ref² ÷ 4`, where D_ref is the largest diameter of any nose cone, body tube or transition still attached to the rocket, pods and strap-on boosters included. This is desktop OpenRocket's "maximum diameter" reference, and the only one the app uses: a `.ork` that asks for a nose-cone or custom reference flies with this one, and the app writes "maximum" back when it saves. Launch lugs, rail buttons, tube fins, inner tubes and rings never set it, however wide they are. After a stage separates, the reference is measured again from what is still attached, so Ar drops at staging if the booster was the widest part. *In the app:* the **Ar — Reference area** column of the flight-data downloads, always in m². A plain Cd override you type on a part or stage is read against this area; a canopy's Cd and a bump's frontal-area Cd each use their own. The same force gives a different coefficient on a different area, so check which area a published Cd uses before you compare it with the app's. [NASA: reference area and drag coefficient](https://www1.grc.nasa.gov/beginners-guide-to-aeronautics/drag-coefficient/).

**Reference length, Lr / reference diameter** — The rocket's reference diameter: the widest nose cone, transition or body tube still attached; launch lugs, tube fins and internal parts do not count. One caliber of stability margin is measured in it, and the circle of that diameter is the reference area the drag and force coefficients are normalized to (see **Reference area, A_ref / Ar**). *In the app:* the **Max diameter** tile in All stats shows it, and the **Lr — Reference length** column of **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** and **⬇ Flight data + charts (.xlsx)** carries it in your length unit, beside **Ar — Reference area** in m²; the **⬇ Drag table (.csv)** carries neither. The **R — Reynolds number** column uses a different length, the nose-to-tail aerodynamic length (see **Aerodynamic length**) — mix the two up and a calculation you rebuild from exported data can be off by a large factor.

**Relative wind** — The airflow the moving rocket meets: the atmospheric wind combined with the rocket's own motion through the air. The angle between the rocket's axis and that flow is its angle of attack, so the wind speed at the pad alone does not give you the angle of attack. *In the app:* nothing is labeled "relative wind"; you see its angle as **Angle of attack** — a flight plot, a column of the flight-data downloads, and **Angle of attack at launch guide exit** in the launch report's details. In a crosswind that angle is why the report's **CP at launch guide exit** can sit forward of your Design tab's CP; in still air the two agree. See **Angle of attack, AoA or α**.

**Reload / single-use motor** — A reload is a set of replaceable propellant and parts that you fire in reusable casing hardware; a single-use motor is built to fire once. Both words describe how a motor is built and used, not its impulse class. *In the app:* for a catalogue motor, the launch report's **Motor type** row, under **Show all details**, gives thrustcurve.org's label — **reload**, **single-use** or **hybrid** (see **Hybrid motor**) — and for most reloads the **Motor case** row names the casing it needs (see **Motor case**). A motor you import from an `.eng` or `.rse` file lists as single-use whatever it is. A reload's catalogue length and weight are for the loaded motor in its casing, and the catalogue leaves open which forward closure that weight assumes; adapters and retainers are in no catalogue. To fly with that hardware, weigh your rocket ready to fly and type the figure into **Weighed pad mass with this motor** — **Weighed pad mass with every motor installed** when more than one motor is loaded, a cluster included — on **Motors & Launch** (see **Weighed pad mass**).

**Reynolds number, Re** — A dimensionless measure of how strongly the airflow's inertia outweighs its viscosity: `Re = V × L ÷ ν`, with V the airspeed, L a chosen characteristic length and ν the air's kinematic viscosity. Faster flow or a bigger object gives a larger Re, other things equal. For the app's skin-friction calculation, L is the active airframe's aerodynamic length, nose to tail, rather than its diameter or a fin chord: `100 m/s × 1 m ÷ 0.000015 m²/s ≈ 6.7 million`, and doubling only the length doubles Re. That is why an enlarged rocket, or one flying in different air, can have a different Cd from the original at the same Mach number. The app treats the boundary layer as fully turbulent at every Re; no value switches it to laminar flow. *In the app:* the **R — Reynolds number** column of the flight-data downloads; the **Drag analysis** panel's **Conditions** menu sets the air its sweep runs in (**Sea level**, **At altitude…**, or, for a design opened from a RASAero file, **File Mach-Alt table**), and Re follows that air at every Mach point; and the **Scale rocket** dialog reminds you that Reynolds number is one thing scaling cannot carry over. [NASA: Reynolds number](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/airplane/reynolds.html).

**RK4 — fourth-order Runge–Kutta** — The numerical method the flight engine uses to step your rocket's motion forward in time: each step samples the changing motion four times — at the start, twice at the midpoint and at the end — and combines them into the next state. "Fourth order" describes how fast the method's error shrinks as the step shortens; it has nothing to do with four directions of movement or four stages of a rocket. *In the app:* RK4 flies the rocket as a rigid body; a descent under a canopy, or a tumbling airframe, is stepped with the simpler Euler method. **Time step (s)** in **Launch conditions** sets the nominal step (blank flies 0.05 s), which the engine shortens wherever your flight gets delicate. See **Time step, dt**.

**RockSim** — A desktop rocket design and simulation program; the app reads its `.rkt` designs and `.rse` motor files. *In the app:* **Open…** reads an XML `.rkt` of up to 3 stages, opening each RockSim simulation that carries motors as a flight configuration (simulations with the same motors fold into one), and **Save As / Export → Save .rkt — RockSim** writes one. An older binary RockSim file is refused: re-save it in RockSim (version 9 writes XML), or export it as `.ork`. A `.rkt` has no nozzle exit field, so a round trip drops that number, and it saves only the configuration on screen — save a `.ork` to keep them all. Import a `.rse` motor with **⬆ Import .eng/.rse** in the motor browser.

**Rod aim** — Which way a tilted launch rod leans relative to the wind. *In the app:* **Rod aim**, beside **Rod angle** in **Launch conditions** and on the phone Fly screen, runs from −180° to 180°: 0° (the default) leans the rod into the wind, 180° downwind and ±90° across it, positive to your right as you face into the wind. It acts only while Rod angle is off zero, and a negative Rod angle already leans the rod downwind. The app's wind always blows from the east, so **Rod aim** is measured from the wind rather than a compass bearing: set it by how your rod leans relative to the day's real wind. Leaning into the wind usually gives the lowest apogee and the least drift; leaning downwind lets the wind stand the rocket back up, so it climbs higher and drifts further.

**Rod angle** — How far the launch rod or rail tilts from vertical. *In the app:* **Rod angle** in **Launch conditions** and on the phone Fly screen, 0° by default, from −30° to 30°. A positive angle leans the rod the way **Rod aim** points — into the wind at the default aim — and a negative one leans it the opposite way. Desktop OpenRocket allows ±60°, so a design file with a steeper rail opens here at ±30°, with an import note quoting the file's own value. See **Launch guide / rod / rail**.

**Rod length** — How far your rocket travels guided by the launch rod or rail before it is free to turn. *In the app:* **Rod length** in **Launch conditions** and on the phone Fly screen, 1 m by default, in your length unit. The engine counts the rocket as clear once it has moved one Rod length from where it started, and the launch report reads **Velocity at launch guide exit** at that moment and grades it on **Lift-off speed OK** (at least 15 m/s, about 50 ft/s). So type the guided travel your rocket really gets, which on a rail can be less than the rail's length. See **Launch-guide exit velocity / rod-exit velocity**.

**Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf)** — The app's default aerodynamics model: desktop OpenRocket 24.12's Extended Barrowman plus a set of additions. "Kbf" names one, the lift the fins induce on the body (see **Kbf / body carryover**); it moves the CP slightly aft and so raises the margin the app shows you — closer on the designs it was validated against, and not automatically safer. The model also adds a sharp-airfoil fin pressure model, the named **Supersonic airfoil** sections, a ×1.8 interference factor on fin skin friction, a pressure-drag floor for a stubby nose, and the power-on base drag and pressure thrust a stage's nozzle exit diameter drives. *In the app:* **Rogers Modified Barrowman (Kbf) — the default** under Preferences → Aerodynamics, and **Rogers Kbf** in the vitals strip's **Aero** selector. See **Aerodynamics model**.

**Roll inertia (loaded)** — Your rocket's moment of inertia about its long axis with the motors in: how hard it is to start or stop the rocket rolling. *In the app:* the **Roll inertia (loaded)** tile in the Design workspace's All stats grid, in kg·m², beside **Pitch inertia (loaded)**, and the **Ir — Rotational moment of inertia** column of the flight-data download. It matters only when something rolls the rocket, such as canted fins. Each inner tube off the centerline adds its mass times its offset squared, for itself and its motor, so a spaced cluster reads higher here than in desktop OpenRocket 24.12 on every model, with no setting to bring 24.12's figure back; an asymmetrically placed inner tube can make the figure read high (7.9 % on one measured layout), because its lateral mass placement is not fully reflected in the computed CG. See **Moment of inertia**.

**Roll rate** — How fast the rocket spins about its long axis: `1 rev/s = 360°/s = 2π rad/s`. *In the app:* the launch report's **Max roll rate** row, under **Show all details**, gives the peak in r/s with °/s alongside, and appears only when the rocket really rolls (above 0.01 rad/s). The **dΦ — Roll rate** column of the flight-data download gives it at every step in rad/s, charted on the .xlsx's **Angular rates** tab. The roll slider beside the 2D view turns only your drawing and has nothing to do with the simulated roll rate. See **Cant angle**.

**Round trip** — Exporting a design to a file format and opening it again. A clean round trip can still lose whatever the format, or the other program, has no place for. *In the app:* **Open…** reads `.ork`, `.rkt` and `.CDX1`; the 3D meshes (`.obj`, `.glb`, `.stl`) and the component tables are export-only, because they hold no editable flight-simulation design. `.ork` keeps the most, so work in it; its camera shroud, protuberance, nozzle exit diameter and weighed pad mass are among the app's own additions, with a parachute's spill hole, the **Supersonic airfoil** fields, **Measured mass & CG** and **Max motor length**, which desktop OpenRocket ignores. A `.rkt` drops the nozzle exit diameter, and a `.CDX1` carries no materials or per-part masses. The note after a save names only some of what those two formats could not hold (an ignition setting or Auto delay a `.rkt` has no way to express, a `.CDX1` rod aim), so a save with no note can still have dropped something: check *Files, Units, and Offline Use → File formats* before you rely on the file.

### S

**Safe deployment** — A screen on how fast your rocket is moving as each recovery device opens; a fast opening zippers a body tube or tears a chute. *In the app:* the **Safe deployment** row of the launch report's **Checks & motor** table, under **Show all details**. It reads **✓ yes** when every device opens at 21.34 m/s (70 ft/s) or less and **⚠ NO** above that; the report's comments call an opening from 21.34 to 27.43 m/s (70 to 90 ft/s) a caution and anything faster a hard opening. The recovery table's **Verdict** column reads **⚠ hard opening** for any device over 70 ft/s, caution band included; the comment line says which band it is in. The first device is judged on the rocket's total speed, a later one on its descent rate. The simulator's own note about an opening over 20 m/s (65.6 ft/s) is a separate, stricter flag and can sit beside a yes.

**Saved run / run history** — One flight's record in the **Saved simulations** table; this browser keeps your newest 500 until you delete them or press **Clear all**. A run records the result: the motor and its delay, the average wind, the density altitude, the headline numbers, the safety verdicts and the aerodynamics model. *In the app:* click a row to open it in the launch report, which tells you whether it still matches your design or what has changed. The design is kept only as a fingerprint, and the motors and launch conditions only to check that match, so a run cannot load them back. The per-timestep data stays out of the run too, so plots come from a re-fly: **📈 Charts** in the row, or **📈 Show charts** on the open run, flies the same flight again without adding a row. Those buttons appear only while your design, motors, launch conditions and aerodynamics model still match the run. The table's **Safe** column shows **⚠** when the lift-off speed, thrust : weight, a deployment speed, an under-stable margin or a descent rate fails; otherwise it shows **△** for an over-stable margin and **✓** when none of those concerns is recorded.

**Scale factor** — The one multiplier, k, that resizes a whole design; in a fully similar scale-up, lengths go as k, areas as k² and volumes as k³. *In the app:* **⤢ Scale…** above the component tree. Type k in **Scale by** (0.01 to 100), or type a **New body diameter** or pick a catalogue tube and k follows, as the new diameter ÷ your design's widest body diameter. Lengths, diameters, wall thicknesses, fin planforms (freeform points included) and axial positions are multiplied by k. Angles, fin and instance counts, material densities, drag coefficients, surface finish, motor choice, deployment settings and launch conditions stay as they are; a camera shroud, rail buttons and a launch lug's bore keep their size and only move to their new positions. With densities unchanged, a solid part's mass goes as k³, a parachute canopy or streamer as k² and cords as k, so a design carrying recovery gear is no longer exactly similar — and its descent rate rises roughly as √k, so size your canopy for the new mass. The dialog checks each scaled motor mount against the standard motor sizes and can put an inner-tube mount on one. Reynolds number and flight performance do not scale with the geometry; the app flies your new size as it is.

**Schematic (2D)** — A flat, true-scale side view of your rocket drawn from the component tree, internal parts included. *In the app:* the **2D** button in the Design tab's **Rocket** panel, beside **3D** and **Aft**. It draws every part at its real size and position, each loaded motor labeled, where the label fits inside the drawn motor, with the name it was loaded under — its common name plus delay, such as `C6-5`, as the vitals strip's **Motor** shows it, and the CG and CP markers with the stability margin between them. Click a part to select it, drag it along its parent to move it, and roll the view with the slider on the left. **📏** toggles the rulers, **⟳ 90°** stands the drawing nose-up, and **⬇ SVG** and **⬇ Image** export it. See *Visualizing the Design → The 2D schematic*.

**Sea-level pressure** — A pressure adjusted to sea level, so weather stations at different elevations can be compared on one scale. Meteorological sea-level pressure and an aviation altimeter setting use different reduction methods. Both match your pad's barometer only at sea level; the higher your pad, the further both sit above what it reads. *In the app:* **Station pressure** in **Launch conditions** wants your pad's own reading, and **☁ Get weather…** fetches that one. A sea-level figure typed at a high pad flies air that is too dense, so apogee reads low; above a 600 m (about 2,000 ft) site the panel cautions you when a typed pressure looks like one. See **Station pressure**. [NWS: pressure definitions](https://www.weather.gov/bou/pressure_definitions).

**Separation event / separation delay** — The trigger, and the extra time after it, that decide when a stage lets go. Ignition, burnout and separation are separate events: a motor can finish its burn well before its stage drops. *In the app:* **Separate at (lower stages)** and **Separation delay** on a **Stage**, and **Separate at** and **Separation delay** on a **Booster (parallel stage)**. You can pick this stage's ejection charge (the default), its motor burnout or ignition, the upper stage's motor ignition, launch, apogee, **Altitude (ascending)** or **Altitude (descending)**, or **Never**, which keeps the stage attached for the whole flight. The top stage ignores the setting. The app has no box for the separation height: an altitude trigger fires at 200 m (656 ft) above the pad, or at the height a `.ork` file you opened carries, so pick an altitude trigger only when that is the height you want. See **Simulation branch**.

**Shape parameter** — A number that picks one profile within a nose cone or transition family. Its meaning depends on the family — for an ogive, 1 is tangent and 0 a cone; for a Haack, 0 is Von Kármán and 1/3 is LV-Haack; for a power series, it is the exponent — and 0.5 is not “half size”: length and diameter are separate inputs. *In the app:* **Shape parameter** beside **Shape**, for Ogive, Parabolic, Power and Haack; it stops a Haack at 1/3. Left blank, the box shows **default: N**, the value it flies. Picking a catalogue part resets it to the shape's default.

**Shock cord / shroud lines** — A shock cord ties your rocket's sections to each other and to the recovery system; shroud lines connect a parachute canopy to its attachment point. They are different parts with different lengths and jobs, and both add to the recovery system's mass. *In the app:* **Cord length** and **Cord material** on a **Shock cord**; **Line count**, **Line length** and **Line material** on a **Parachute**. Each weighs its length times the line material's mass per meter (times the count, for shroud lines), and neither adds drag.

**Shock wave** — A thin region where pressure, density and flow speed jump, found in compressible flow. Shocks can form locally in the transonic range before the rocket as a whole reaches Mach 1, and the drag they bring helps explain the drag rise around Mach 1. *In the app:* you see their effect as wave drag — the **Pressure / wave** line in the **Drag analysis** panel's **By type** breakdown, where the rise starts near Mach 0.9.

**Shoulder / coupler** — A shoulder is the part of a nose cone or transition that fits inside the next tube; a coupler is a separate sleeve joining two tube sections. Both sit hidden inside the airframe: they count for construction and mass, and stay out of the outer shape the air flows over. *In the app:* **Shoulder diameter**, **Shoulder length** and **Shoulder thickness** on a nose cone; **Fore shoulder diameter**, **Fore shoulder length** and the matching aft pair on a transition; and the **Tube coupler** part. A shoulder's weight is its wall. On a nose cone, type a **Shoulder thickness**: left blank, the box shows **default: 0** and the shoulder weighs nothing. **Fit shoulder to tube ⌀** on a nose cone sets its shoulder diameter to the bore of the body tube behind it. A catalogue pick fills in the shoulder's diameter and length but not its thickness; when the catalogue row carries a mass, the pick enters it as the whole part's weight, shoulder included. A transition's shoulders always fly weightless, because the app gives them no wall thickness. If either one matters to your CG, weigh the part and enter its mass as an override.

**Shoulder end capped** — A shoulder closed at its far end by a disc, making it a sealed cup rather than an open tube. *In the app:* the **Shoulder end capped** tick box on a nose cone, and **Fore shoulder end capped** and **Aft shoulder end capped** on a transition. On a nose cone the cap is a disc of the part's own material, as thick as the shoulder wall and as wide as its inside, at the shoulder's aft end. With a **Shoulder thickness** typed, it adds mass and moves your CG, and it adds no drag; left blank, the thickness flies 0 and the cap weighs nothing. On a transition the boxes are saved with your design but change nothing in the flight (see **Shoulder / coupler**).

**Simulation branch** — The recorded flight of one piece flying on its own: once a booster lets go, it gets a branch separate from the sustainer's. A branch belongs to one staged simulation — neither another batch candidate nor a second run of the whole design. *In the app:* the launch report gives each dropped stage or separating booster its own **separate flight:** line, with its apogee, landing rate and recovery table, and **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** adds its columns to the same file under its stage name, with their own time column. A ring of strap-ons flies as one branch with one verdict.

**SI units** — The International System of Units: meters, kilograms, seconds and newtons. The app's physics works in SI throughout. *In the app:* Preferences → **Units of measure** sets how you type and read each quantity — one by one, or all at once with **Metric defaults** or **Imperial defaults** — and every value converts to SI before it flies. Changing units changes the numbers you see, never the size or mass of your design.

**Six degrees of freedom, 6DOF** — Three directions of movement plus three of rotation: pitch, yaw and roll. *In the app:* the term appears only in this guide. Your rocket flies as a 6DOF rigid body in free flight; on the launch rod or rail it only slides along the rod; and once a recovery device opens, the app switches to a simpler point-mass (3DOF) descent that follows position under gravity and drag but ignores how the assembly tumbles. An airframe that goes unstable with no recovery device out goes to a separate tumble model. See *How It Works: Physics & Math → How a flight is simulated*.

**Skin-friction drag** — Drag from the air's viscous rub along every surface exposed to the flow; it depends on Reynolds number, compressibility, roughness and wetted area. *In the app:* the **Friction** line in the **Drag analysis** panel's **By type** breakdown. The app treats the boundary layer as fully turbulent, and each part pays whichever friction coefficient is higher: the smooth-surface value set by the Reynolds number, or a roughness value set by that part's **Surface finish**. So your finish sets a floor, not a cap: a smoother finish lowers friction drag only down to the smooth-surface value, and the smoothest finishes (**Polished (0.5 µm)**, **Mirror surface (0 µm)**) usually already sit there, so choosing between them changes nothing.

**Slender body / strake** — A slender body is long compared with its width; a strake is a long, low lifting surface running along a body. *In the app:* a **Camera shroud / fairing** flies as a slender strake: its side outline goes to the simulator as a one-panel fin, whose low-aspect-ratio lift reduces to the classic Jones slender-body model, so it moves your CP the way a fin set does under whichever aerodynamics model you choose. That covers its normal force only — the wake it sheds onto the fins behind it is left out. See *Designing the Rocket → Camera shrouds (fairings) — how they're modeled*.

**Snapping** — Pulling a part onto a meaningful position as you move it close to one, so it lands where it would sit in a real build. *In the app:* drag a part along its parent in the **2D** view, or drag the **Offset** slider under **Position (in parent)**, and it snaps to the parent's ends and middle, to each neighboring part's leading and trailing edges, and to the front and rear edges of a fin's tab, where centering rings usually sit. The pull reaches a few pixels in the drawing and 1.5 % of the parent's length on the slider; typed offsets and arrow keys land exactly. Around the body, **▲ on a fin** and **⟂ between fins** set **Angle around body** in one click.

**Solid (filled)** — A nose cone or transition made of solid material all the way through, like a turned balsa nose, rather than a hollow shell. *In the app:* the **Solid (filled)** tick box on a nose cone or transition. Ticked, the part weighs its whole volume at its material density and its **Wall thickness** stops counting; unticked, it is a shell of that wall. A solid part can weigh several times as much as the hollow one, so check this box before you trust your CG. Picking a catalogue part sets the box to match that part.

**Specific impulse, Isp** — Total impulse divided by the propellant's weight: `Isp = I ÷ (m_propellant × g₀)`, usually given in seconds. It tells you how much impulse each unit of propellant weight buys, rather than how big the motor is or how long it burns. *In the app:* the app does not show Isp; the motor browser lists **Impulse (Ns)** and **Burn (s)**. [ThrustCurve: motor statistics](https://www.thrustcurve.org/info/motorstats.html).

**Speed of sound, a** — How fast a small pressure disturbance travels through the air. It depends mainly on temperature, so Mach 1 is no single speed. *In the app:* the simulator works it out from the air temperature at each altitude with a straight-line fit, 165.77 + 0.606 × T (T in kelvin) — about 340.4 m/s at 15 °C — and divides your airspeed by it for the Mach number. A hot pad raises it: at 35 °C (95 °F) it is about 352.5 m/s.

**Spill hole / vent** — An opening at the center of a parachute canopy. *In the app:* **Spill hole ⌀ (0 = none)** on a parachute. The app takes the vent's area out of the drag coefficient it flies — Cd × (1 − (hole ⌀ ÷ canopy ⌀)²) — and stops the hole at 95 % of the canopy's diameter; how a vented canopy inflates or oscillates is left out. The launch report's recovery table shows you both figures, such as `1.44 (1.50 less a 122 mm vent)`. See *Designing the Rocket → Parachute spill holes*.

**Stability margin / static margin** — The distance from the CG back to the CP, measured against the rocket's size. With positions measured aft from the nose, `margin in calibers = (CP − CG) ÷ D_ref`. A positive margin puts the CP behind the CG, so a disturbed rocket swings back to point along its flight path; a negative margin reverses that. *In the app:* the vitals strip's **Stability**, the stats readout on the Design canvas and the callouts on the **2D** and **3D** views read in calibers, or as `100 × (CP − CG) ÷ aerodynamic length` percent if you choose **% of length** (**Both** shows the two side by side) under Preferences → Display → **Stability shown as**. The **Stability** tile in **▤ All stats** always reads calibers, with **Stability (of length)** beside it in percent. The app screens it against a 1–3 caliber band, labeling a margin below it **under-stable** and above it **over-stable**. Those labels are a screening rule, not proof of a safe or unsafe flight: margin changes in flight, and dynamic response, wind, launch-guide exit conditions and model limits matter too. The launch report grades the margin as your rocket leaves the launch guide, where a crosswind moves the CP forward of the Design tab's still-air figure. A model that puts the CP further aft shows more margin, and that alone does not make it the conservative answer.

**Stage / serial staging** — A stage is an assembly with its own role in the rocket's propulsion and separation sequence. Serial stages sit one above another along the main axis, and each can have its own motors, ignition, separation and recovery. *In the app:* **+ Add stage** under the component tree adds a booster stage below your current ones; each **Stage** carries its own separation trigger, and each staged motor its own **Ignition** on Motors & Launch. Contrast with **Parallel booster / strap-on booster**.

**Stall** — The loss of the expected lift as the flow separates from a surface at a large angle of attack. *In the app:* the simulator caps each fin's normal force at a 20° stall angle — past it, more angle of attack buys no more fin force — in place of a stall model for each fin shape. Past 17.5°, before any recovery device opens, it flags the flight: a stable rocket gets the note “Large angle of attack — the rocket flew notably sideways to its path”, and an unstable one switches to tumbling. Treat any result at a large angle of attack as an approximation, however stable the small-angle numbers look.

**Standard deviation, σ** — A measure of how far values spread around their average; for wind, the typical size of the speed swings above and below the average. *In the app:* **Wind gusts σ** beside **Wind avg** in **Launch conditions**. A forecast's maximum gust is a different statistic, so it cannot set σ by itself; once you apply the wind from **☁ Get weather…**, **Estimate from forecast gust** (**Estimate from reanalysis gust** for a date the ERA5 reanalysis answers) offers σ ≈ (gust − average) ÷ 3, capped at the average wind and good to about ±25 %. Every flight uses one fixed gust history, so σ gives you one plausible gusty flight, not a spread of landings. See *Launch Conditions → Wind gusts from a forecast*.

**Standard sizes** — The common motor casing diameters: 6, 13, 18, 24, 29, 38, 54, 75/76, 98, 132 and 152 mm. Some catalogue motors come in other diameters, 10.5, 20, 32, 64, 81 and 161 mm among them. A scaled mount rarely lands on a standard size: an 18 mm mount scaled by 2.27 has a 40.9 mm bore. *In the app:* in **⤢ Scale…**, each inner-tube motor mount has a **Mount size** menu — **Scaled size**, **Nearest standard**, the **Standard sizes** list or **Custom…**, where you type the bore yourself for one of the other diameters — and a tick box snaps every mount to the nearest standard size, keeping its wall thickness. Sizes too wide for the tube around the mount are grayed out; a body tube that is itself the mount keeps its scaled size. Where strength decides it, pick the size yourself rather than the nearest.

**Station pressure** — The air pressure at your launch site's own elevation, with no correction to sea level: what a barometer sitting on the pad reads. *In the app:* **Station pressure** in **Launch conditions**. Left blank, it flies the standard pressure for your **Site altitude** — the grayed number in the box. A typed value is taken as the pad's pressure, so its kind matters as much as its units: at a 3,900 ft pad a barometer reads about 878 hPa where a weather app says about 1,013, and the sea-level figure flies air about 15 % too dense, so apogee reads low. **☁ Get weather…** fetches the station pressure for you. [NWS: station pressure](https://www.weather.gov/bou/pressure_definitions).

**Streamer** — A long strip of material that slows your rocket's descent by fluttering in the airflow; its length, width and material set its drag, and unlike a round parachute it has no canopy diameter. *In the app:* a **Streamer** part with **Strip length**, **Strip width**, **Canopy material** and **Drag coefficient (blank = auto)**; left blank, the coefficient comes from the strip's size and material, and the report's Cd column shows **—**. The descent treats a streamer as an effective drag area, so treat a very long, narrow one's descent rate as an estimate.

**Subsonic / transonic / supersonic** — Slower than the local speed of sound / the band around Mach 1 where subsonic and supersonic flow exist side by side over the rocket / faster than the local speed of sound. Transonic is a range rather than one exact Mach value. *In the app:* **Max Mach** in the launch report's details. **Auto** selects **Supersonic** above Mach 0.9, where the Barrowman models start to lose accuracy — one fixed rule for choosing a model, the same for every rocket whatever its shape. Fly past Mach 0.9 on Rogers Kbf or Classic Extended Barrowman and a warning above your results offers **Try Auto & re-fly (this session)**. [NASA: flight regimes](https://www.grc.nasa.gov/www/k-12/airplane/mach.html).

**Supersonic (aerodynamics model)** — The app's extended aerodynamics model, with high-speed treatments of body and fin forces, wave drag and related effects. Choosing it applies that model at all speeds of your flight, subsonic parts included. *In the app:* **Supersonic** in Preferences → Aerodynamics, the setting that persists, or in the vitals strip's **Aero** selector, for this session only. Its name is no claim that every supersonic design or plotted Mach value is validated equally well: it is checked against wind-tunnel data to about Mach 4.6, its peak drag through Mach 0.95–1.2 reads low, so on a flight through Mach 1 its apogee can read high — the app can show more altitude than the rocket reaches — and above Mach 10 it extrapolates. See **Auto (aerodynamics)**.

**Surface finish / roughness** — How rough an exposed part's surface is, stored as a roughness height for the drag calculation. *In the app:* **Surface finish** on each exposed part: nine choices from **Rough (500 µm)** through the default **Regular paint (60 µm)** to **Mirror surface (0 µm)**, and **→ all** beside the label gives every part that has a finish the same one. A rougher finish can raise skin friction; the smoothest can only bring it down to the smooth-surface value. Finish is separate from your display color and leaves the boundary layer fully turbulent whatever you pick. Scaling a design keeps each finish, so a downscale is proportionally rougher.

**SU (single-use)** — thrustcurve.org's code for a single-use motor: one supplied loaded and fired once, where a reload fires in reusable casing hardware. *In the app:* the app spells it out — the **Motor type** row in the launch report's details and the **Type** column of the run-table download read **single-use** (or **reload**, or **hybrid**), and a `.ork` save writes desktop OpenRocket's `single`. A motor you import from an `.eng` or `.rse` file lists as single-use whatever it is. See **Reload / single-use motor**.

**Sweep** — How far a fin's outer part sits forward or aft of its root. *In the app:* **Sweep** on **Trapezoidal fins** is a distance: how far aft of the root's leading edge the tip's leading edge sits, negative for forward. Aerodynamics texts often give a sweep angle instead; convert through the fin's height (sweep distance = height × tan(sweep angle)) before you type one in place of the other.

### T–Z

**Terminal velocity / settled descent rate** — The steady speed a falling rocket settles at once drag balances its weight: `v = √(2·m·g ÷ (ρ·Cd·A))`. It takes time to reach after deployment, and it eases as the air thickens on the way down. *In the app:* the **Recovery sizing** panel sizes canopies for this rate in your launch site's air — about 8 % faster at 5,000 ft than at sea level. The launch report's **Descent after** and **Landing descent rate** are each read at one instant — touchdown, or just before the next device opens — so a device that opened only moments earlier may still be settling toward its rate.

**Three degrees of freedom, 3DOF** — Motion tracked as a point: position in three directions, with no orientation. *In the app:* when a recovery device deploys, your flight switches from **Six degrees of freedom, 6DOF** to a 3DOF descent — gravity and drag on a point, in strides of up to 0.5 s whatever your **Time step (s)** setting. A rocket or spent booster tumbling with nothing deployed falls on a tumble model, also a point. Descent figures leave out swinging, spilling and canopy dynamics, and a parachute with no Cd of its own flies at 0.8. See *How It Works: Physics & Math → How a flight is simulated*.

**Thrust / average thrust / peak thrust** — Thrust is the motor's push at one instant, in newtons. Average thrust is the total impulse spread over the burn time; peak thrust is the curve's largest value. *In the app:* the **Thrust** plot and the flight-data download's **Thrust** column show the push as flown. The number after the letter in a designation such as C6 is the average thrust. A motor with over 80 N of it, or over 160 N·s of total impulse, counts as high power, and a booster flying one with no recovery device draws a warning. The thrust at guide exit, which **Thrust : weight at departure** uses, can be far from that average. [ThrustCurve: motor statistics](https://www.thrustcurve.org/info/motorstats.html).

**Thrust curve** — A motor's thrust recorded against time; its shape decides how the impulse is delivered. *In the app:* every motor flies from a thrust-curve file — one bundled from thrustcurve.org (a motor that **↻ Check thrustcurve.org** adds downloads its curve on first use), or an `.eng` or `.rse` of your own that you import through **🔎 Browse motors / import EX (.eng, .rse)…** — interpolated linearly between its samples, with no combustion model. A curve more than 5 % off the motor's certified total impulse draws a note when you load it. Under **Rogers Kbf**, **Auto** and **Supersonic**, a stage with a **Nozzle exit diameter** — which the app fills in by itself for many AeroTech and Loki motors — gains pressure thrust while its motor burns, more as the air thins, so the **Thrust** plot sits above the file's curve even when you typed nothing. See **Pressure thrust**.

**thrustcurve.org** — The free online database of rocket motors: certified figures, and thrust-curve files from certifying bodies, manufacturers and users. *In the app:* the app ships its catalogue of {{MOTOR_COUNT}} motors, pulled {{MOTOR_DB_DATE}}, with every curve it publishes for them ({{CURVE_MOTORS}} have one), so choosing a motor needs no network. The **Motor database** dialog's header names the source and the catalogue's age, and its **↻ Check thrustcurve.org** button lists what has changed since, keeping the result in your browser. Of several curves for one motor, the app prefers a file whose time points all run forward, then one whose burn time is within 15 % of the catalogue's, then one whose total impulse is within 3 % of the certified total, and then a certifying body's file over a user's or manufacturer's upload. A motor with none flies only from a file you import. See *Motors → The database and browser*.

**Thrust-to-weight ratio (Thrust : weight, T:W)** — Thrust divided by the rocket's weight: `TWR = F_thrust ÷ (m × g)`. Both are forces, so it has no units; divide by the mass in kilograms instead and the figure comes out about ten times too high. *In the app:* with **Show all details** open, the launch report gives **Thrust : weight at departure**, as the rocket clears the guide, and **Thrust : weight OK** checks it against 5 : 1. The same figure is the optional **Thrust : weight** tile (**⚙ Choose metrics**), the **T:W** column of the batch table and of the run-table and batch downloads, and what Batch simulate's **Min thrust:weight** criterion grades. The flight-data download's **Twr — Thrust-to-weight ratio** column gives it at every time step. A value at one instant describes only that instant; thrust changes all through the burn.

**Time step, dt** — The interval a numerical simulation advances by in one stride. *In the app:* the **Time step (s)** field under **Launch conditions** (blank = 0.05 s, range 0.01–1 s) sets the longest stride the main integrator takes from lift-off until a recovery device deploys or the rocket tumbles; on the guide, the limit is a fifth of your setting. The simulator shortens strides wherever the motion or an event calls for it, down to a twentieth of your setting, and may stretch one by up to that twentieth to land exactly on an event. Once a recovery device is out, or the rocket or a spent booster tumbles, a simpler stepper takes strides of up to 0.5 s whatever you set. The flight-data download's **dt — Simulation time step** column records each stride. Below 0.05 s the panel says what the setting costs in run time, and it buys no accuracy you can read. The time step is separate from elapsed flight time, and a smaller one leaves an inaccurate mass, thrust curve or aerodynamic assumption just as wrong. See *Launch Conditions → Time step — what it sets, and why the default is 0.05 s*.

**Transition** — A body section joining two different diameters, its fore and aft ends sized independently. It can widen toward the rear, narrow as a **Boattail**, or make another shaped change; its shoulders slide into the neighboring tubes and are separate from the exposed profile. *In the app:* **Transition**, placed in the chain of body parts of a stage, a pod set or a strap-on booster, with **Length**, **Fore diameter**, **Aft diameter**, **Wall thickness**, **Shape** (Conical when unset), **Shape parameter**, **Solid (filled)** and a shoulder at each end. Of the fin types, only **Freeform fins** attach to a transition.

**Tube coupler** — A short tube inside the airframe, usually where two body tubes join, and often the shell of an avionics bay. *In the app:* **Tube coupler** goes inside a **Body tube** and can hold a **Bulkhead**, a **Centering ring**, a parachute, streamer, shock cord or mass component. You set **Length**, **Wall thickness** and **Material density**; its outside diameter is automatic, the inside of the tube it sits in. Being internal, it adds mass and moves the CG but carries no drag. For the shoulder on a nose cone or transition, see **Shoulder / coupler**.

**Tube fin** — An open tube mounted along the outside of the rocket as a stabilizing surface, in place of a flat plate fin. *In the app:* **Tube fins**, added to a body tube, described by **Fin count** (1 to 8, the most the simulator flies), **Length**, **Outer diameter** and **Wall thickness**; the tubes are spaced evenly, and **Rotation (about body axis)** turns the whole set. A blank **Outer diameter** shows "auto: …", the size at which neighboring tubes just touch — with fewer than three tubes, the body diameter. Once you enter a size, **Fin count** stops at how many tubes of that size fit around the body (a larger count you type is stored as that many), and with three or more tubes the box refuses a size above the just-touching one. The gap between tubes follows from the outer diameter, the count and the body diameter, so it has no field. A tube fin's outer diameter is separate from the main body diameter.

**Tumble / tumbling** — Falling end over end with nothing deployed, slowed by the airframe's own drag as it turns broadside. *In the app:* with no recovery device out, an unstable rocket past 17.5° angle of attack, or a dropped booster with nothing making lift, switches to a tumble model: a falling point whose drag comes from the fins' and body's side-on areas. The launch report marks such a booster **(tumbles)**. If motor thrust is still over 0.01 N, the affected flight branch stops instead, with an explanation beginning **The rocket began to tumble while the motor was still burning**. See **Ballistic descent**.

**Turbulence intensity** — Wind-speed standard deviation divided by mean wind speed, often given as a percentage. It measures fluctuation relative to the mean, which is a different thing from an added steady wind. *In the app:* you enter the two parts — **Wind avg** and **Wind gusts σ**, the standard deviation in wind-speed units — and the intensity is their ratio. A `.ork` that stores only an intensity, the older form, imports as σ = intensity × average. The simulated wind wanders about the average as pink noise, always blows from the east — **Rod aim** sets how your rail leans against it — and is the same at every height. A `.ork` flying a multilevel wind profile is flown on its average-wind settings, and the import note says so. See **Wind avg / Wind gusts σ** and **Pink noise**.

**Validation / verification** — Validation compares predictions with physical evidence under stated conditions; verification checks that a calculation or program does what it was built to do. *In the app:* the verification is a differential test you never see on screen; the guide describes it under *How It Works: Physics & Math*. It runs the same cases through the app's own kernel (OpenRocket 24.12's Java source with the app's patches to it) twice, once on a Java VM and once as the JavaScript compiled from it, and requires bit-identical output. That verifies the compile; it does not tell you whether any answer matches desktop OpenRocket. The **Supersonic** model, listed in Preferences → Aerodynamics as validated to Mach 4.6, was checked against NASA ARCAS wind-tunnel and Basic Finner free-flight data — validation, for those shapes. Only your own flights validate a prediction for your rocket.

**Velocity / vertical velocity** — Velocity carries speed and direction. Vertical velocity is its up-and-down part: positive going up, negative coming down, and zero at apogee even while the rocket still moves sideways. *In the app:* the **Velocity** plot, the launch report's **Max velocity** and the flight-data download's **Velocity** column give speed over the ground, wind drift included. Vertical velocity is the download's **Vz — Vertical velocity** column, and the report's **Descent after** and **Landing descent rate** are vertical rates. For speed through the air, see **Airspeed / air-relative velocity**.

**Viscosity** — A fluid's resistance to shearing motion; air has it too, though it feels far less sticky than a liquid such as oil. Dynamic viscosity, μ, and **Kinematic viscosity, ν** (μ divided by density) are different quantities, so use the one your Reynolds-number formula asks for. *In the app:* there is no viscosity field. The simulator works viscosity out from the air's temperature and density at each step, for the Reynolds number that sets skin-friction drag — the flight-data download's **R — Reynolds number** column.

**Wake** — The disturbed air behind a body or appendage, which can reach surfaces farther aft. *In the app:* the wake a **Camera shroud / fairing** or **Protuberance (drag bump)** sheds onto a fin is left out: that fin flies at full free-stream dynamic pressure. When a shroud or bump sits within 20 of its own heights ahead of a fin set and near one fin's line, a warning under the Design drawing says so. It gives the direction of that one term — on its own, it moves the CP forward, toward less margin — and no size, because no measurement sizes it. **⟂ between fins** beside the part's **Angle around body** clocks it between the fins, which removes the question. Tube fins are outside this check.

**Wave drag** — Pressure drag from compressibility: the shock and expansion pattern around a shape at transonic and supersonic speeds. Nose profile, fin thickness, sweep and airfoil shape affect it, and it is distinct from skin-friction drag. *In the app:* the Drag analysis panel's **By type** breakdown carries it in the **Pressure / wave** line, which climbs from about Mach 0.9, beside **Friction**. **Classic Extended Barrowman** holds its wave-drag data flat past Mach 2–4; **Supersonic** works wave drag out shape by shape and is the model to pick for a fast flight, but its peak drag through Mach 0.95–1.2 reads low against wind-tunnel data, so on a flight through Mach 1 expect its predicted apogee to read high. See **Shock wave**.

**Weathercocking** — A rocket's tendency to turn into the wind as it leaves the guide. It usually lowers apogee and moves the landing spot. A large static margin strengthens it; wind, guide angle, speed and the rocket's rotational response matter too. *In the app:* with **Show all details** open, the launch report's **Weathercocking** row grades it from the average wind divided by the guide-exit speed: under 0.1 low, under 0.25 moderate, otherwise high, in red. A margin above 3.0 cal reads **△ over-stable (caution)** on the **Static margin** row. With a tilted rod, **Rod aim** 0° leans it into the wind, and letting the rocket weathercock from there usually gives the lowest apogee and the least drift.

**Weighed pad mass** — A scale reading of this complete rocket ready to fly, with the selected motor or motor set installed. It belongs to that design and flight configuration alone. *In the app:* **Weighed pad mass with this motor**, under the primary mount's motor on **Motors & Launch** (**Weighed pad mass with every motor installed** when more than one flies). The app subtracts the airframe's dry mass — your measured one if you entered it, otherwise the computed one — and the catalogue motor masses, and carries the difference as inert hardware on the primary mount's motor for the whole flight; the line under the field shows the arithmetic: what you weighed, less the dry mass, less the catalogue motor. A weight lighter than dry mass plus catalogue motors, or a difference heavier than the airframe, is refused and nothing is carried; a difference over half the motor's own weight flies but is flagged. Load a different motor on the mount you weighed with, or unload it, and the field is blank for the new motor. Change another mount's motor or a cluster count and the number is kept but grayed and left out of the flight until you restore the set or reweigh. Reweigh too when hardware such as a forward closure changes. See **Primary mount**.

**Wetted area** — The surface area the airflow washes over, used in skin-friction calculations. It differs from head-on frontal area, fin planform area and the reference area behind the rocket's Cd: a long tube can have a large wetted area while keeping the same frontal area. *In the app:* there is no readout; wetted area sits inside the drag the Drag analysis panel's **By type** breakdown plots as **Friction**, and each part's **Surface finish** sets how rough it is.

**Wind avg / Wind gusts σ** — The mean wind speed, and the standard deviation of the gusts about it. *In the app:* both sit under **Launch conditions**, 0 by default; **Wind avg** is also on the phone's **Fly** screen. The simulated wind always blows from the east. **☁ Get weather…** can fill **Wind avg** from a forecast's 10 m wind, or from the ERA5 reanalysis for an older date. While **Wind avg** still holds that value and the gust is more than 0.1 m/s above it, a row below offers **Estimate from forecast gust** (**Estimate from reanalysis gust** for an ERA5 date): σ ≈ (gust − average) ÷ 3.0, capped at the average wind and good to about ±25 %. Every flight uses one fixed gust history, so σ gives you one plausible gusty flight rather than a landing spread. See *Launch Conditions → Wind gusts from a forecast*.

**Workspaces** — The pages the app is split into, chosen with the workspace tabs — under the header on a wider screen, docked along the bottom on a phone. *In the app:* **Design** holds the component tree, drawings and property panel; **Motors & Launch** holds motors, delays, batch simulation and **Launch conditions**; **Results** holds the launch report, plots, drag analysis and saved runs. On a phone-width screen a fourth, **Fly**, comes first and is where the app opens; wider screens hide its tab. On every workspace but Fly, the vitals strip shows **Rocket**, **Stability**, **Mass**, **Recovery**, **Motor** with **⏏ Unload**, **Aero**, **Apogee** after a flight, and **Launch**. Launching takes you to Results, and a reload returns you to your last workspace. See **Fly screen**.

**Zippering** — A tear down a body tube caused by recovery loading concentrated at its edge, often through the shock cord; a fast deployment raises the risk. *In the app:* with **Show all details** open, the launch report's **Safe deployment** reads **⚠ NO** when a device opens faster than 70 ft/s (21.3 m/s); its comments call 70–90 ft/s a caution — "watch for a zippered tube" — and anything faster a hard opening. The recovery table's **Verdict** column reads **⚠ hard opening** for any device over 70 ft/s, caution band included; the comment line says which band it is in. The simulator separately notes any opening above its fixed 20 m/s (65.6 ft/s). These screen the speed only; the tear strength of your build is yours to judge. See **Safe deployment**.

### File and app terminology

| Term | Meaning in this guide |
|---|---|
| Autosave | The app's automatic copy of your working session — design, motors, flight configurations and launch conditions — kept in this browser and restored the next time you open the app, even after you close the tab or the browser crashes. All your open tabs share one autosave, and a tab asks before it overwrites another tab's changes. It is a convenience: the durable copy of your design is an `.ork` you saved. |
| `.CDX1` | RASAero II's design file (**Save .CDX1 — RASAero II**). It holds geometry, surface finish, motors, nozzle exits and the author's stated launch weight and CG, but no materials or per-part masses: walls default to 2 mm. Where the file states a launch weight and CG, each stage's mass and CG are pinned to those figures, with the motor backed out, so your loaded rocket weighs and balances as the author said. Whatever the file leaves unstated, and any stage the import note lists as not applied, keeps the 2 mm-wall figures. Either way, how the mass spreads part by part is made up, so treat its altitudes as provisional. |
| CSV | Comma-separated values: a plain-text table. The app writes six: the component table (**Export .csv — component data**); the **Saved simulations** run table (**⬇ Run table (.csv)**, one summary row per run); the batch results (**⬇ CSV** in **Batch simulate motors…**, the run table's columns, one row per batch flight); one flight's every timestep (**⬇ Flight data (.csv)**); the drag analysis's coefficients by Mach (**⬇ Drag table (.csv)**); and, from the preset picker, every row of one part type that your search and manufacturer filter match (**⬇ CSV**). The parts list is the one the app reads back: **⬆ CSV** in the preset picker adds to or updates your own presets. All six are data exports; keep your design as an `.ork`. |
| DXF | A CAD drawing-exchange format for flat outlines. **✂ DXF (CNC/laser, 1:1)** on a selected fin set, centering ring, bulkhead, coupler or engine block writes one part per file (for a fin set, a single fin with its tab) as AutoCAD R12 in millimeters; cut the **CUT** layer alone. It holds the outline only — no airfoil shaping, cant, materials, motors or recovery. |
| `.eng` / RASP | The plain-text motor file most simulators share: a header line (name, size, delays, masses), then time–thrust pairs. It describes a motor, not a rocket: **⬆ Import .eng/.rse** in the motor browser adds it to your library under manufacturer **EX**. A `P` delay, or one of 90 s or more, means plugged; with no per-sample mass in the file, burn-off is spread in proportion to impulse. |
| glTF / GLB | A 3D scene format; GLB is its single-file binary form. **Export .glb — 3D model with colors** writes the rocket's outside surface with your component colors as real materials, in meters, nose at the origin — for Windows 3D Viewer, PowerPoint, Blender and web viewers. |
| Local storage / site data | The space your browser keeps for one website — here your autosaved design, **Saved simulations**, preferences, imported parts, imported EX motors and cached thrust curves, in one browser profile on one device at one address, with nothing synced. On an iPhone or iPad, Safari and the Home Screen app keep separate stores even at the same address. Clearing cookies and site data removes all of it and the app's offline copy; files on your disk stay. A browser may also clear it on its own when space runs short or the app goes unused. See *Installing, offline, and saving your work → What happens if I clear my browser cache?* |
| Mesh | A surface of connected points, edges and faces, usually triangles — what the 3D view draws and the `.obj`, `.glb` and `.stl` exports hold. The flight model works from each part's own dimensions, material, finish and overrides rather than from any mesh, so a mesh changes no simulated number, and a printed part can differ from what flies: printed fins are flat prisms, with the airfoil left to sanding. |
| OBJ | A 3D geometry format for modeling, rendering and CAD tools. **Export .obj — 3D geometry** writes the rocket's outside surface as the 3D view draws it, in meters with the nose at x = 0 — scale it on import if your tool expects millimeters. It is an open visual mesh with no simulation data; for a printable part use **🖨 STL for printing (mm)**. |
| `.ork` | OpenRocket's design file, and your working save: **Save .ork — OpenRocket design** writes components, materials, overrides, motors, flight configurations and launch conditions. The app's own additions — among them camera shroud, protuberance, nozzle exit diameter, weighed pad mass, a parachute's spill hole, the **Supersonic airfoil** fields, **Measured mass & CG** and **Max motor length** — are ignored by desktop OpenRocket. Your **Saved simulations**, imported parts and an imported EX motor's thrust curve stay in the browser, so keep that motor's `.eng` or `.rse` beside the `.ork`. |
| PWA / installed web app | The app added from your browser as its own window or Home Screen icon. Installing is optional; an ordinary page load is what makes the app work offline. On Chrome, Edge and Android Chrome the installed app shares the browser tab's storage. On an iPhone or iPad the Home Screen app is a separate copy with its own storage, and on the one iPad tested for this guide it would not open offline — use Safari for field work there. Firefox has nothing to install. See *Installing, offline, and saving your work → iPhone and iPad: read this before you take one to a launch*. |
| `.rkt` | RockSim's design file, whichever program wrote it — RockSim, desktop OpenRocket or the app. The app reads the XML `.rkt` of RockSim 9 and later, and refuses an older binary RockSim file with a message to re-save it. **Save .rkt — RockSim** holds up to 3 stages, the format's limit. A round trip drops what the format, or the app's reader and writer, cannot carry: the nozzle exit diameter, a camera shroud's drag, and a part the app has no equivalent for, such as a ring tail (the import note names it). The save writes the loaded motors as one RockSim simulation and leaves out your launch conditions, your other flight configurations and your **Measured mass & CG** figures; keep those in an `.ork`. |
| `.rse` | RockSim's XML motor file — a motor like `.eng`, imported the same way, and a different thing from a `.rkt` design. The app flies its thrust points. It flies the per-point masses only when the file turns RockSim's own mass model off (`auto-calc-mass="0"`) and they start at the propellant or the loaded mass (the import note says when they start at neither); with the model on, as in every `.rse` checked, burn-off is spread in proportion to impulse. It reads the nozzle exit, `exitDia`, in millimeters, and a stage flying that motor takes its **Nozzle exit diameter** from it; a value under 15 % or over 75 % of the case diameter is dropped with a note. The throat (`throatDia`) and each point's measured CG go unread: the motor's CG is the middle of its length. |
| Saved simulation | One flight kept in the **Saved simulations** table on Results — its summary numbers, motor and conditions, one row per run, the newest 500 kept. It lives in your browser apart from the design, and saving or opening an `.ork` leaves it untouched, so keep the table with **⬇ Run table (.csv)** or **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)**. See **Saved run / run history**. |
| Share link | A web address carrying a whole design — what **Save .ork — OpenRocket design** would write — packed after the `#`, the part of an address browsers keep off the network, so the design reaches no server. It is a snapshot from the moment you copied it with **Save As / Export → 🔗 Copy share link**. Some chat apps cut a long link short; send the `.ork` then. Like an `.ork`, it names an imported EX motor without its curve. |
| STL | A triangle-mesh format for 3D printing. It carries shape only, so the app cannot open one as a design, and it states no unit of its own: both of the app's STLs are in millimeters, so set your slicer to millimeters. **🖨 STL for printing (mm)** on a selected part writes a watertight solid ready to slice; a fin set gives one fin, as a flat prism with its tab. With a printer set under **Preferences → 3D printing**, a part that will not fit it in one piece but can be cut turns that button into **🖨 STL for printing — N pieces**, which downloads a ZIP of segments and a README. **Export .stl — 3D shell (reference)** writes the whole rocket as an open display model. |
| SVG | A scalable vector drawing. **⬇ SVG** on the 2D side view prints the drawing and its data header at true scale; **📐 Fin template (SVG, 1:1)** on a selected fin set is a cut template with a 50 mm calibration ruler. Print at 100 % and measure the ruler: any other length means your printer scaled the page. |
| Vitals strip | The instrument strip above the workspace tabs on Design, Motors & Launch and Results; the phone's Fly screen has its own pad readout instead. It shows **Rocket** (the name), **Stability**, **Mass** (loaded, with motors), **Recovery** (the mass under the chute), **Motor** (the primary motor, **+N** for the others, and **⏏ Unload**), the **Aero** selector, **Apogee** of your last flight — with a ⚠ when the design, motors, weighed pad mass, launch conditions or aerodynamics model have changed since that flight (hover the apogee to see which) — and the **Launch** button. A model picked in **Aero** lasts for this session; **Preferences → Aerodynamics** is the setting that persists, and the strip shows **↺** to go back to it. An **M+** beside **Auto** means Auto has switched this design to Supersonic. |
| XLSX | An Excel workbook, numbers stored as numbers under a frozen, filtered header. The app writes four: **Export .xlsx — component data**, **⬇ Run table (.xlsx)**, the batch results' **⬇ XLSX**, and **⬇ Flight data + charts (.xlsx)**, which adds live Excel chart tabs covering every column. All four are data exports; keep your design as an `.ork`. |

### Symbols and abbreviations in plots and exports

The two downloads in the **Flight plots** heading on the Results tab, **⬇ Flight data (.csv)** and **⬇ Flight data + charts (.xlsx)**, head the twelve headline series with plain names and units: Time, Altitude, Velocity, Acceleration, Mass, Thrust, Drag force, Mach number, Stability margin, CP location, CG location and Angle of attack (for example **Altitude (m)**; Mach number has no unit). Every other column is headed by symbol, name and unit, for example **Vz — Vertical velocity (m/s)**, and a dimensionless one by symbol and name alone, for example **Cd — Drag coefficient**. Each column comes in your Preferences unit for its quantity, except a few fixed ones: time and dt in s, thrust and drag in N, the stability margin in cal, the rates dΦ, dθ and dΨ in rad/s, φ and λ in degrees, Il and Ir in kg·m², and Ar in m². In a staged flight's CSV, each booster's columns start with the stage name and carry their own time column; the XLSX gives each stage its own data sheet instead. The symbols t, h, Vt, At, m, Ft, Fd, M, S, Cp, Cg and α are the simulation kernel's own names for the twelve headline series, and desktop OpenRocket uses the same symbols as variables in its custom expressions. Your export headers and the flight plots use the plain names instead. On screen you meet only two of the symbols: t, in the plot readout **t (s)**, and M, in the **Cd (M0.3)** tile of the **All stats** drawer.

Use this table to read your columns; a given flight may carry fewer series than it lists. A blank cell means "not computed at that step", not zero. CP, the stability margin S and the coefficients Cn, Cθ, CτΨ, Cτs, CτΦ, CfΦ, CζΦ and Cζθ stay blank while the rocket is still on the launch guide. When a recovery device deploys, or the rocket tumbles, the simulation switches to a simpler descent model that moves the rocket without turning it (see **Three degrees of freedom, 3DOF**). From then on those coefficients, the angle of attack α and the rates dΦ, dθ and dΨ are blank again, and several columns keep a number whose meaning has changed: CP reads 0; the stability margin reads a negative number (the CG position in calibers); Cdf and Cdb read 0; Cd, Cdp and Cda all become the drag of whatever is slowing the rocket down (the canopy, or the tumbling airframe) divided by the rocket's own reference area; and Θ and Φ hold the values they had when the descent model took over. When you read CP, S and the coefficients, use the powered and coasting flight only.

| Symbol or label | Read as |
|---|---|
| `t`, `dt` | Elapsed simulation time (exported as **Time**); the length of the simulation step at that row, which varies as your flight runs. |
| `h`, `ha` | Altitude above the pad (exported as **Altitude**); altitude above sea level, which adds your **Site altitude**. |
| `Vt`, `Vz`, `Vl` | Total speed relative to the ground, climb or fall and sideways drift combined (exported as **Velocity**); vertical velocity, positive up; lateral (horizontal) speed. Under a canopy **Velocity** includes the wind drift, so read your descent rate from Vz. |
| `At`, `Az`, `Al` | Size of the total acceleration (exported as **Acceleration**); vertical acceleration, positive up; size of the lateral (horizontal) acceleration. |
| `Px`, `Py`, `Pl` | Position east of the pad; position north of the pad; horizontal distance from the pad (**Lateral distance**). |
| `θl` | Lateral direction: the compass bearing of your rocket from the pad, clockwise from north (90° = east), paired with the lateral distance Pl. |
| `Θ`, `Φ` | Vertical orientation (Θ): the angle of the rocket's axis above the horizon — +90° with the nose straight up, as on a vertical rod, 0° when level and −90° with the nose straight down. Its header, **Θ — Vertical orientation — zenith**, borrows desktop OpenRocket's word, but the angle is measured up from the horizon, so straight up reads 90° where a true zenith angle reads 0°. Lateral orientation (Φ, headed **Φ — Lateral orientation — azimuth**): the compass direction the nose points toward, clockwise from north (90° = east). While the rocket points straight up it is undefined and reads 0. Under a canopy or while tumbling, both hold the values they had when the descent model took over. Both symbols are uppercase; the lowercase φ below is latitude. |
| `φ`, `λ` | Latitude and longitude of the rocket, starting from your **Latitude (°)** and **Longitude (°)** fields (east positive; a blank longitude flies −80.6°). Lowercase φ is latitude; uppercase Φ is orientation. |
| `m`, `mp` | Rocket mass (exported as **Mass**); motor mass (**mp — Motor mass**). In your export, `mp` is the whole motor, casing included, not the propellant alone. |
| `Ft`, `Fd` | Thrust (exported as **Thrust**); the drag force the simulation reports (exported as **Drag force**). |
| `M` | Mach number (exported as **Mach number**). |
| `R` | Reynolds number, headed **R — Reynolds number**; this glossary writes it Re (see **Reynolds number, Re**). The simulation builds it on the rocket's aerodynamic length, a different length from Lr. |
| `S` | Stability margin in calibers: (CP − CG) ÷ reference length, worked out at every step from the in-flight CP at that moment's Mach and angle of attack and from the CG at that moment's mass. It moves during the flight: the CG moves as propellant burns, and the CP moves with speed and angle of attack. So it can differ from the Design tab's still-air figure (Mach 0.3, zero angle of attack, pad mass). The symbol S itself appears nowhere: the plot panel and the CSV/XLSX column are both labeled **Stability margin (cal)** and always read calibers, whatever you choose under **Stability shown as**. The launch report's **Static margin at launch guide exit** is this series read at that moment, shown in the unit you pick under **Stability shown as**. It is blank while the rocket is on the launch guide. After a recovery device deploys, or once a separated booster tumbles, the simulation stops computing a CP, so the column reads a large negative number (the CG distance ÷ reference length, negated). Ignore that number: it measures the CG position alone. |
| `Cp`, `Cg` | CP and CG locations (exported as **CP location** and **CG location**), measured from the nose tip in your **Rocket dimensions** unit. Here `Cp` is a position; in other aerodynamic writing the same notation often means the pressure coefficient. |
| `α` | Angle of attack (exported as **Angle of attack**): the angle between the rocket's axis and the air flowing past it. In the first rows of a windy flight, before the rocket has speed, the crosswind alone can make it read up to 90°. The guide also uses α for the wind model's spectral exponent (α = 5/3), an unrelated quantity. |
| `Twr` | Thrust-to-weight ratio at that step, thrust ÷ (mass × g); it reads 0 after burnout. |
| `Cd`, `Cdf`, `Cdp`, `Cdb` | Total drag coefficient and its friction, pressure and base parts. `Cd` also carries any overridden drag, which the three parts leave out: a Cd override you type, and the drag the app assigns to a protuberance or a camera shroud. So while the rocket flies on its own aerodynamics, the three parts add up to `Cd` only on a design without overrides; with one, `Cd` reads higher than their sum. Under a canopy or while tumbling, `Cdp` carries all of `Cd` and `Cdf` and `Cdb` read 0. |
| `Cda` | Axial drag coefficient: drag along the rocket's own axis. It equals `Cd` at zero angle of attack and moves away from it as α grows. |
| `Cn`, `CNα` | Normal-force coefficient; normal-force slope, how fast the normal-force coefficient Cn grows with angle of attack. CNα is a property of the design, a different quantity from Cn: you meet it as the `cna_per_rad` column (per radian) of the Drag analysis panel's **⬇ Drag table (.csv)**. |
| `Cθ`, `CτΨ`, `CτΦ` | Pitch, yaw and roll moment coefficients. |
| `Cτs` | Side-force coefficient. |
| `CfΦ` | Roll-forcing coefficient: the roll drive from canted fins. The `f` stands for forcing, as in forcing moment, rather than friction. |
| `CζΦ`, `Cζθ`, `CζΨ` | Roll, pitch and yaw damping coefficients. Your export carries `CζΦ` and `Cζθ` only: the simulation leaves yaw damping unrecorded, so `CζΨ` is absent. |
| `dΦ`, `dθ`, `dΨ` | Roll, pitch and yaw rates. |
| `Il`, `Ir` | Longitudinal moment of inertia, for pitch and yaw; rotational moment of inertia, for roll. Both are about the CG and fall as propellant burns; at launch they equal the **Pitch inertia (loaded)** and **Roll inertia (loaded)** tiles you see in the **All stats** drawer. |
| `Lr`, `Ar` | Diameter-based reference length, in your **Rocket dimensions** unit; reference area. Lr is a different length from the aerodynamic length used in Re. |
| `Vw`, `θw` | Wind speed, in your **Wind speed** unit, and the direction the wind blows from, clockwise from north. The app's wind always comes from the east, so θw reads 90°, flips to 270° at any instant a gust drives the wind below zero, and reads 0 in a calm. The wind is the same at every height: **☁ Get weather…** applies a single 10 m wind to all of them. |
| `T`, `P`, `ρ`, `Vs` | Air temperature, air pressure, air density and speed of sound at the rocket, each in your unit for that quantity; air density uses your **Bulk density** unit. |
| `g`, `Ac` | Gravitational acceleration at the rocket's position; Coriolis acceleration from Earth's rotation. |
| `N`, `N·s` / `Ns`, `Pa`, `mbar`, `hPa` | Newton; newton-second, the unit of total impulse — the motor browser's **Impulse (Ns)** column writes it `Ns`, and the certified-impulse note and this guide write `N·s`, the same unit; pascal; millibar, the pressure unit of the start-up units and of both **Metric defaults** and **Imperial defaults**, so the unit of your pressure fields and the exported `P` column until you change it; hectopascal, used in the **Station pressure** help. `1 mbar = 1 hPa = 100 Pa`. |
| `rad`, `°`, `r/s` | Radian, degree, and revolutions per second. `r/s` means revolutions, not radians: the launch report's **Max roll rate** row shows it with °/s beside it, and the run-table downloads head the column **Max roll rate (r/s)**. The flight-data export gives roll, pitch and yaw rates (dΦ, dθ, dΨ) in rad/s. `π rad = 180°`; `1 r/s = 2π rad/s = 360°/s`. |
| `OD`, `ID` | Outside diameter and inside diameter; the part labels in a .dxf cut profile use both. |
| `AGL`, `ASL`, `ISA` | Above ground level, above sea level, and International Standard Atmosphere. |
| `LE`, `TE`, `6DOF` | Leading edge, trailing edge, and six degrees of freedom. The first two appear in the fin fields **LE chamfer length**, **TE chamfer length** and **LE bluntness radius**. |
| `NACA`, `NASA`, `AGARD` | National Advisory Committee for Aeronautics (NASA's predecessor until 1958); National Aeronautics and Space Administration; Advisory Group for Aerospace Research and Development. In the guide they mostly name published sources: data the models are built from (NACA Report 1307, NASA TR-R-100) and measured data the supersonic model is checked against (NASA's ARCAS tunnel tests, the AGARD HB-2 standard model). They are sources, not certifications of the app. When you pick **NACA (round LE)** in a fin set's **Supersonic airfoil** selector, you choose a shape: a NACA-family section with a rounded leading edge. Its nose radius comes from the fin's thickness and chord, so the **LE bluntness radius** field, which the other named sections use, is ignored for it; and like every named section in that selector it is ignored by **Classic Extended Barrowman**. A RASAero file's "Subsonic NACA" section imports as the **Airfoil (pointed)** cross-section. |
| `NAR`, `TRA`, `TMT` | National Association of Rocketry, one of the bodies that certify motors in the US; the guide cites its motor-testing manual. Tripoli Rocketry Association and Tripoli Motor Testing: two abbreviations the app leaves unused, though the guide names Tripoli for its Class 3 documentation packets and its motor-test letters. The app does not show you which body certified a curve. When a motor has several published files, the app ranks them by soundness, then by agreement with the catalogue's burn time, then with its total impulse, and only then prefers the one thrustcurve.org marks as a certification file. |

Three abbreviations the app's own labels leave unused: `AoA`, angle of attack (the app writes **Angle of attack** or α); `MMT`, motor mount tube, which you will see in some manufacturers' part numbers and descriptions in the list that **📦 Choose from preset database…** opens (see **Inner tube / motor mount**); `q`, dynamic pressure, ½ × ρ × V², a different quantity from the air pressure `P` that the app neither plots nor exports (see **Dynamic pressure, q / max Q**).
